- 2026-10-20 — **GISTDA's hexagons keep their violet but are drawn far brighter, with an edge** (owner: "change the color of GISTDA's flood seen polygons to something easy to notice both on Satellite + Terrain map and also Night terrain map"). Fill `#9944FF` by day and `#BB77FF` at night, with a deep violet edge by day and a pale one at night. The hue is the one chosen on 13 Oct and for the same reason - green, yellow, orange, red and blue are the five alert levels, grey is the "not updated" marker, the flood-hazard classes are a pink ramp, and teal and slate are the brand - but the shades of that build were too deep to find on either background. Magenta was tried first and rejected: it is a measured 90 units from every alert colour but sits inside the hazard ramp's pinks. A test holds the colour that distance from all of them and holds it bright enough for the background it is drawn on. — The one colour nothing else on this map is allowed to use is worth keeping; what was wrong was its value, not its hue.
- 2026-10-20 — **The map's background follows the sun alone, never the theme somebody picked by hand** (owner: "the basemap should flip only follow the time rule, for example if the user click change to dark theme when it's on day time, the basemap should remain for the light theme, only switch to night terrain when the sun is set"). The page now carries both: `data-theme` is what colour the app is, and `data-daylight` is whether the sun is up where the reader is, written by the same pre-paint script and kept current by `DaylightTheme`. `lib/map.ts` reads the second (`nightNow`, which `darkNow` was renamed to), so the basemap, our own layers' ink and the pins on them all follow the daylight. This supersedes the 18 Oct arrangement, where picking a theme turned the district's daylight photograph into a night map. — The theme is about reading a screen in a dark room; the background is about recognising the ground outside the window, and the two are not the same question.
- 2026-10-20 — **Safe places are kept true by place checks, split from "verified", and no check ever changes a level** (owner: "somehow can you come up with ability that the users in all roles can verify the safe places maybe another sub interface"; proposed, and the three open calls answered by them through AskUserQuestion). Until now a safe place's whole truth was written by one admin through `admin_safe_place_update`: a verified authority unit standing in the shelter car park could not change a thing, and a resident had no way to say anything at all. So the record was only ever as fresh as the last time an admin touched it, and the Module 2 score — elevation, distance, past extents — cannot know that the door is locked or the road is cut. The design separates the two facts that `verification_status` was flattening into one word: **is the record true** (it exists, right spot, capacity about 200, there are toilets, the ground floor went under in 2024) which is structural and keeps for a season, and **is it good right now** (open, full, water inside, road passable) which perishes in hours. They are stored apart and shown apart, and the perishable one carries a time and an author on the face of the card, because "verified" on a resident's screen reads as *safe to go* and a safe place is never a verdict. Everything is kept in one append-only table `place_checks` — a row is never updated, since three people saying "full" at 14:00 is itself the evidence and the history makes the audit trail free — and `safe_places` carries only the latest check's time and standing. **The crowd never flips the badge, in either direction:** the card shows the latest official line (an admin, or a verified unit covering that tambon) and, separately and attributed, what residents have said, never merged and never with the word verified, because a false "closed" turns people away from a dry shelter and a false "open" sends somebody into a flooded one, so neither may apply itself. What residents' checks do instead is show as their own line, in the page's own ink with no alert colour — the same shape as the 2026-10-13 decision that a resident's flood report counts at once and says who is speaking — and raise a flag in the authority and admin queues. **Freshness is a marker, never a downgrade:** each place carries when its condition was last confirmed and a window that tightens with the alert level in force at its tambon (seven days at normal, 24 h at watch, 6 h at warning, 2 h at evacuate); past it the card keeps its status and gains a grey "not checked since 14:20", exactly as safety rule 3 treats a stale alert. Three screens read the one table: a resident's single button on the safe place card, an authority round list at `/authority/places` ordered oldest check first with a two-tap confirm, and the flag-and-correction queue on `/admin/safe-places`. Against the safety rules: a check never computes or publishes an alert and never notifies a resident, so rule 2 holds and flags reach only admins and teams; the point a check was sent from and its author are personal data and are read under RLS like a report, the public seeing only the aggregate line and never who checked (rules 5 and 6); nothing in the flow goes near the SOS path (rule 1); a check queues offline and a queued one never renders as though it landed (rule 7); and unlike an SOS a check may be rate-limited and refused outright, because refusing one costs nobody their life. Three calls were the owner's. (1) **Timing: the thin slice before launch, the rest after** — the freshness marker and the authority round list ship now, so the teams the launch post is recruiting have a pre-season sweep to do (walk the shelters in your tambon once before November, confirm capacity and toilets and whether the ground floor flooded), which is also the answer to 2026-10-18's "why has no unit registered": there was nothing yet for a registered unit to do. The resident flow, the aggregate line, the admin queue and the printable door QR become their own phase after 25 October. (2) **Only signed-in residents may send a check**, not anonymously as an SOS or a flood report may be sent: a check is not a cry for help, so the safety argument for no door at all does not apply, and an identity behind each one is worth more than the few extra checks anonymity would bring. The consequence, stated and accepted: the door QR on a shelter wall now costs the mosque committee member a one-time LINE sign-in before their first check, which is two taps and then remembered. (3) **Residents' checks do show on the public card**, attributed and never as the badge.
- 2026-10-20 — **The airport operator is described on "Who built Jaga", not named** (the owner, after reading the page: use “National Airports Holding company” instead). Their own portfolio names the client; Jaga's page does not, because this repository is public and the owner did not choose to publish their current client here. **Nothing the reader can weigh is lost**: the scale of the work (27 airports, a 13-module portal used by five operating companies and the regulator, delivered on the client's existing systems with no new licences) is the part that makes the step mean something, and it is unchanged. The name has also been taken out of the entry above, which is the one thing this log does not normally allow - old entries are never edited. The decision recorded there is untouched; only the client's name was replaced by what it is, because leaving a name in a public file after being asked to drop it would defeat the point of being asked. — A page about a person should carry what they chose to publish about themselves, and nothing more.
- 2026-10-20 — **Risk & Live Map is rebuilt for a phone: the map first, the choosers behind two pills, every explanation folded under it** (a tester, passed on by the owner: it is "not easy to see or use" on a phone; three ways out were drawn for them and they chose C: "phone map C"). Measured on the live site at 360 x 640 in Thai before anything was designed: the page was **4,683 px — 7.3 screens — of which the map was 384 px (8%), and it did not begin until 1,146 px down**, with 912 px of choosers above it and 2,142 px of key, readings and reservoir legend below. The cause was not any one block: the laptop console approved on 16 October has rails either side of its map, and on a phone those rails have nowhere to go but one block on top of another, so the phone never got a design — it got the console unstacked. Four changes, all below the console's breakpoint, with **nothing taken off the phone**: the map goes to the top and is given exactly the room the measured header (156 px) and the sticky hotline bar (93 px) leave, which is 391 px and is why a taller map would only hide its own 2D/3D buttons behind the hotlines; the hazard, the province and the six layer switches wait behind two pills that say what the map is showing and open one chooser at a time above it, capped so the map is never pushed off the screen while they are used; what a tap answers with opens directly under the map, which gives up height for it so both are on the screen at once; and the key, the reservoir's path, the method and the outlook are all folded. Result: **2,239 px — 3.5 screens — with 375 px of map one short scroll from the top.** Three smaller calls that each came from a measurement, not a preference. (1) The map's own "all four provinces", "reset" and "my places" buttons are **drawings only below 640 px**, their words still read out: labelled, they wrapped onto two lines and covered 180 px of a 375 px map. (2) Bringing a card into view on a phone brings back **the map, not the card** — putting the card's own top under the header, which is what a laptop wants, would push the map clean off the screen to show a card already on it; the card still takes the keyboard. (3) The frozen copy of the chip row no longer appears while the chips are folded away, and in this arrangement will rarely appear at all, since the chips now sit beside the map rather than above a long page. Against the safety rules: the four hotlines are never covered, and the "back to the map" pill rides above them; the alert levels keep icon, word and colour inside a folded key that is one tap from the map (rule 3); the four hazards still to come and the provinces Jaga does not cover still say so in the chooser (rule 10); and nothing here touches the laptop console, the home screen, or what the page does without the map (rule 7). — A phone is how almost everyone in these four provinces will meet Jaga, and it was the one screen that had been given somebody else's layout.
- 2026-10-20 — **The console's head carries a small turning Earth with a bronze satellite, in place of the 19 Oct downlink strip** (the owner: the strip "takes too much space on the Header", redesign it after the Airbus MilSatCom picture, "make the earth spinning slowly, and the Satellite floating and getting data"; through AskUserQuestion they chose a round globe icon, muted bronze foil, real dotted continents, and laptop, tablet and phone if it fits). Supersedes the 2026-10-19 strip entry. The globe is about 2.75 rem on a laptop and 2 rem on a phone or tablet; the whole piece is 1.75 times that wide, sits right after the heading on the console and at the end of the title's line elsewhere, and was measured to add no height on any screen (the 1360 px gate is gone). Land is **Natural Earth's 1:110m land (public domain)**, sampled on rings 3° apart by `scripts/globe-dots.mjs` into 1,326 dots in under 1 KB (`lib/globe-dots.ts`), drawn in a true orthographic view on a canvas 15 times a second, and only while it is on screen and the tab is open. The satellite's foil is **bronze, not gold**, because yellow is the Watch level and orange is Warning; a unit test holds every foil colour more than ΔE 20 from both. When Jaga's service area turns under the satellite, the place glows and data runs up the beam for about a fifth of each turn. Asked for less motion, it holds that frame with the link lit. It stays decoration that says nothing: no figure, no word, `aria-hidden` (safety rule 8). The picture is our own drawing in the style of Airbus's; nothing of theirs is copied. — The head is for the heading; the decoration should be the size of an icon, not a banner.
- 2026-10-20 — **A resident checks a safe place from the place's own pin on the map, and what they say never becomes the badge** (owner, the same day the thin slice landed: "from each safe place point on map also can be the good place where the normal users can verify the places"). This brings forward the half of the morning's place-checks decision that was deferred to after launch, because the reason given is the right one: the pin is where people already look at a shelter, and the person who knows first that the hall is full is standing in it and will never register as a rescue team. The entry point is therefore on the map pop-up **and** on the card, the same component in both, so there is one way to answer and not two. **The constraint is the whole design, and it is enforced in the database rather than in the screen:** `place_check_resident` writes a row in `place_checks` and does not touch `safe_places` at all, so a resident's answer cannot move the status however many people send it — a false "closed" turns people away from a dry shelter and a false "open" sends somebody into a flooded one, so neither direction may apply itself, and only an admin or a team covering that tambon writes the status. What a resident's check does instead is two things that matter. It shows as **its own attributed line under the status** ("3 people here said closed"), in the page's own ink with no alert colour, counted as **distinct people and not rows** so one person repeating themselves counts once — the same shape as the 2026-10-13 decision that a resident's flood report counts at once and says who is speaking. And it reaches **the covering team's round list, above even a place nobody has ever visited**, because two or more people disagreeing with the card is the thing a team can act on today; that is the queue, and it is read by the people who can act, which is why a separate admin queue was not built now. A dispute needs **two independent accounts**: one person is shown but never called a dispute, because one person is not a crowd. Resident answers are worth showing for **twelve hours, or the place's own shorter window** under a warning or an evacuate (`resident_say_window`), since a crowd reading a situation goes out of date faster than a team's record. Signed in only, as chosen earlier the same day, and **rate-limited twice** — ten an hour per person and once per place per half hour (`system_settings.place_check_limit`, so it can be changed without a release) — the first against somebody working through the whole map, the second against one person saying the same thing repeatedly to look like many; unlike an SOS a check may be refused outright, because refusing one costs nobody their life. A visitor who is not signed in is offered the way in and told in one line what it is for, never a form that fails at the end. The map's pop-up leaves the water, the road and the note out and keeps the three answers, which is the part that matters when standing outside. Nothing here issues an alert or notifies anybody (safety rule 2), and the public answer carries counts only, never an identity, a position or free text (rules 5 and 6). One thing was changed for cost rather than correctness: the three words about the road are copied from the flood report's namespace into `placeChecks` and pinned equal to their source by a test, because the home screen and the map were otherwise going to carry all 47 report keys on a slow connection to use four of them.
- 2026-10-20 — **On the laptop console the weather bar's clouds keep to its open end** (the owner: "make animated the cloud from the weather on header be in empty space on the right side mainly, since it's empty there"). `WeatherScene` takes `cloudLane="end"`, used only by `ConsoleSky`: the clouds enter over the end edge and fade out before the middle, in the lower two-thirds of the bar so they pass under the temperature rather than behind it, measured at 50-92% of the width at 1280, 1440 and 1920 px. The veil over the sky is unchanged, so every word on the bar keeps its 4.5:1. The card on /weather keeps its clouds crossing the whole card. — The start of the bar is where the title and buttons are; the end is open sky.
- 2026-10-20 — **The two readouts that open show everything they are counting, and the card walks through the rest** (the owner, of the river-gauge and reservoir cards on the console: "for the case that there are 3 guages that says '3 near the bank', when clicked it should show the worst 3 first, regardless they are near or not, also the map should zoom the extents of the 3 not only one, also make it blip for 3 times. Same should work for the dams card... I want them to be able to click next to see different one as well in the detail section"). Three things follow from it. (1) **A river at or near its bank now comes first however far away it is**, which supersedes the rule of 18 October where the person's own rivers were the pool and risk only decided inside it: the readout counts all four provinces, so what it counts is what it opens. Their own places still break a tie, and still come first for everything below the bank. (2) **A press frames and lights every mark it is pointing at, at most three** (`LIT_MAX`): the gauges at or near their banks, worst first, or the reservoirs spilling or above the level they are normally held at — and where none is, the one the card opens on, so a press never points at nothing. `flashDam` carries codes rather than a code for this. (3) **Previous and next inside the opened card** (`CardSteps`), over a total order so Next cannot wander and a second press cannot land elsewhere, each button naming the station or reservoir it will go to; stepping lights that one alone and does not scroll the panel again, because the reader is already looking at it. Nothing here reads as a level: the marks are lit in the water colour, never an alert colour (safety rule 2), and three pulses about one every 1.2 s stay far below the three a second that can harm a photosensitive reader. — What a card counts and what it opens should be the same thing.
- 2026-10-20 — **"Who built Jaga" keeps its content out of this repository altogether** (the owner, after the page was built: "make sure, to not bring anything in this page to public repo"). The page stays public on the site; its source does not live here. The copy, the portrait and the employers' marks sit in the private data repo and in a new public `site-content` bucket, and `scripts/fetch-content.mjs` brings them down during `prebuild` into `messages/about/` and `public/about/`, both gitignored. **It fetches at build time and never at request time**, because this is a flood-warning app: a page that waits on a bucket is a dependency in the request path, and a dependency in the request path is a risk on every other page too. After the build they are ordinary static files costing a visitor exactly what they cost before. A build that cannot reach the bucket does not fail - the namespace is missing, the page has nothing to show, and a fork gets the same. The code that stayed had to be emptied as well: the career steps were keyed by employer, the addresses were the owner's own pages and the portrait was named after him, so those travel with the content too (`road.order`, `check.at`, `portrait`) and this repository names no employer, no address and no face. The page's own tests moved with it and **skip themselves where the content is absent**, so CI holds the code while the owner's machine holds the page to its rules - no phone number, no shouted brand, no link to the repository. **Two things are deliberately left undone**: the six commits already pushed still carry the old content and the photo, and purging them needs `git filter-repo` and a force-push to `main`, which waits until after the 25 Oct launch when the tree is quiet (it is on the tracker's Reminders); and the design reference to a European aerospace firm in the console's animation entries above is left alone, because it is about how a strip should look and not about anybody's employment. — The owner chose to publish their career on their own page; publishing it again in a repository they keep public for the code is a different decision, and not one they made.
- 2026-10-20 — **The resident's check is asked for on the pin only, and where no team covers the tambon it writes the status itself** (owner, answering the three things left open: "1. pin only", "2. yes keep it to go to the team that covers the area, if no team then should directly update, no check", "3. if it costs money for me, then no need to have that feature"). Three decisions. **(1) The ask lives behind the pin.** The map's pop-up asks, and so does the card that opens from it; the home screen's top-three list no longer does, because it added three folded buttons to a screen already measured as too long on a phone. What residents have said still appears on every card: that is a reading, not an ask, and it costs no height. **(2) Where no verified team covers the tambon, what the person standing there says becomes the status.** This supersedes, for unteamed tambons only, the rule set earlier the same day that a resident's check can never move the status; the owner's reason is the right one, that a dispute reaching nobody is wasted and the card would otherwise stay wrong for the whole season. Two things were added to keep it honest rather than merely automatic. The status is written as `checked_source = 'resident'`, so the card reads "reported by someone there at 14:20" and never implies a team or Jaga confirmed it, and a later team or admin check overwrites both. And **a place a resident closed stays in the top three**: `rank_safe_places` drops closed places, so without that change one person could take the only shelter in an unteamed tambon off the list entirely, with no team to put it back - the dangerous interaction of this whole feature, and the reason the "closed" test there now asks who closed it; a place closed by an admin or a team is still dropped, as before. Both writes are in `audit_log`. One thing deliberately not done: **the screen never says whether this place has a team**, because coverage is readable only by teams and admins (spec 9), so the wording states the rule - what happens where there is no team - and the card shows the result. The threshold is **one person**, which is the literal instruction and is what makes it useful in a sparse tambon where two reports of the same shelter may never arrive; the provenance line is what makes that defensible, and it can be raised to two by one condition if the owner prefers. **(3) The printable shelter-door QR costs no money** - it is generated locally with the same `segno` the launch poster used, and it is a sheet of paper - so the feature was not dropped for cost; what it costs is a one-time sign-in by the person scanning it, which was what the earlier note meant. It remains unbuilt and parked until the owner asks for it.
- 2026-10-20 — **Approved: the console's clouds kept to the open end of the weather bar, as built** (the owner: "the current one is good"). The two offers made with it are declined: the veil over the sky stays the same everywhere (no lighter corner to make the clouds show more), and the weather readout keeps its words even where a long condition wraps it to a second line. — Settled by the owner after looking.
- 2026-10-20 — **The live pulse sits on the folded readout, and closing a card puts the map back** (the owner, two notes on the console: "these 2 cards should show the blinking situation light on the default folded card, no need to hover on to see the blinking light", and "like the GISTDA details, when clicked on close button should bring the map to the default extent"). The pulse was in the part of the card that only opens on hover, which is exactly the glance it is there for; it is now beside the figure on both cards that open, and the reservoir's card has one for the first time. It pulses **only while the readings behind it are current** — no station reporting, or a dam feed marked stale, and there is no dot — because a dot on a stopped feed would say "live" about figures that are not; and it still says nothing about what the figures mean, since a measurement is not a level (safety rules 2 and 3). The close button now follows the satellite layer's arrangement: the frame the map was actually showing is remembered the first time a readout moves it, and closing the card goes back to it, so stepping through three stations still returns to where the reader started. A card that did not move the map does not move it on the way out — a tap on a tambon is not answered with travel. — The glance the card is for must not need a hover, and a view you were taken from is a view you should be brought back to.
- 2026-10-20 — **The globe's satellite is silver-white and teal, not bronze** (the owner: "make the animated satellite match more with our theme like one of your choices and you used to recommended", then "make sure it looks great on both light and dark theme"). Supersedes the bronze-foil choice of the same day's globe entry. The hull is silver-white, outlined in the brand slate (#1D3B53) with a teal (#2F9C95) band; the solar panels stay dark with light-teal (#7FD1C9) edges. By day a soft slate shadow and a deep-teal dish rim keep the silver visible on the pale page; after dark a faint teal glow and a light-teal rim. Checked in both themes at four widths. Unit tests keep every hull shade more than ΔE 20 from each level's colour (stale excepted: it is a marker, not a level) and the trims on the brand tokens. — Brand slate and teal are decoration colours by rule, so the craft now belongs to the page instead of standing apart from it.
- 2026-10-20 — **Approved and public: "Who built Jaga" at `/about`** (the owner, after reading it: "about ok. Build it"). Everything in the entries above stands as built: the career as a line with each step ending on the part of Jaga it produced, the animated journey across three industries, the honest split of what the founder did and did not do, the portrait cut out and standing in the page's own light, the employers' marks with a line saying whose they are, and the four "why" paragraphs in the owner's own account - from the region, but not there for the last two floods, which is why the app has to work without waiting for him. Two things carry the page: **a third tab on the nav that held the science and the system**, worn by the page itself so a reader can go back, and **a third quiet link at the foot of every other page** beside "What Jaga can do" and "How Jaga works". Both are drawn **only where the content is**, and the route 404s without it, because that content is not in this repository and arrives with the build: a fork gets two tabs and two links rather than a tab leading nowhere. The review address `/dev/about` is gone. The nav's label, which said "these two pages", says "these pages". **Still outstanding and deliberately so**: the Malay is the English until round 2 of the translation, and the history purge of the six commits that still carry the old copy waits for the quiet after the 25 Oct launch. — The page a sceptic needs is the one the app was least willing to write; having written it, hiding it behind a review address would have been the only wasted part.
- 2026-10-20 — **What a tap answers with is never boxed: on a phone the card takes the height it needs and the map gives up most of its own** (the owner, after using the morning's rebuild: "when clicking at buttons of Dam or any buttons that lead to show more detail, the detail space is too small, because most of the space already dedicate to the map... it should shrink the map smaller so it give more space to the details"). Two faults, one of them mine from a few hours earlier. The card had been given a window of 45% of the screen with its own scrollbar — and **the reservoir's card is 3,961 px of figures, drawing and history, the sea's 2,483**, so that window was a letterbox showing 4% of it. Worse, the window was not even the 45% asked for but **169 px**, because the map under it kept the 320 px floor (`min-h-80`) it carries everywhere else and simply refused to shrink, so the two together overflowed the screen. Fixed by removing the cap rather than enlarging it: the card is now a block of its natural height and **the page scrolls**, which is how every other long thing in Jaga is read, and the map keeps a strip of 40% of the free height (150 px on a 360 x 640 phone) so the reader can still see the reservoir, gauge or tambon the card is about, lit where the map put it. Because the card can now be twenty screens long, **its "close" is pinned under the header for as long as the card is on the screen** (`order: -1`, sticky at the measured `--jaga-header`, at the end of the line so it covers the ends of words rather than their beginnings), and the existing "back to the map" pill still rides above the hotlines. The frame drawn around the card in the morning is gone: with the card no longer a small box it was a card around a card. The alternative the owner was offered and may still take is taking the map away entirely while a card is open; the strip was kept because a figure about a reservoir is worth less when you cannot see which water it is. — A card with twenty screens of content does not want a bigger window; it wants no window.
- 2026-10-20 — **"About" in the header replaces the "?", and /about carries its own share card** (the owner: "Add about page in the header next to Account. In there user can find about, what jaga can do and how jaga works as well", and "If I share this page link anywhere, it should show as Who built Jaga not as standard App's thumbnail"). The header gains a word beside Account, and the **"?" that led straight to "What Jaga can do" (16 Oct) is removed to pay for it**: measured in Thai at 360 px, a fourth word takes the header from 156 px to 185 px and wraps all four labels onto two lines; with the "?" gone it is 156 px again and nothing wraps. So the header has **one informational way in rather than two**, and it is the one the owner asked for: the About page's nav leads to the science and the system, and its closing block to all three. "What Jaga can do" is one tap further than it was, from that page and from the quiet line at the foot of every page, which is unchanged — this supersedes the 16 Oct decision and is a line to put back if the owner misses the shortcut. The share card is drawn **from the page's own content** (portrait, heading, name, role, three figures) so the two can never drift, and it is a **JPEG at 54 KB** rather than a 456 KB PNG, because a chat app fetches it before it shows anything. The page spells out its `openGraph` and `twitter` metadata in full rather than inheriting, since page metadata merges with the layout's and the app's picture would otherwise stand. — A link to a page about a person should show the person; and a header with two doors to the same room has one door too many.
- 2026-10-20 — **On a phone the map in the page takes two fingers, and one tap gives it a screen of its own where it takes one** (the owner, after living with the rebuilt map: "when scrolling down, while on the map, when we intend to scroll down passing the map, if touching within the map, it just move the map instead of scrolling pass it... fix this problem with a clever way"). It is the oldest complaint about any map inside a page that scrolls, and it was named in the measurements that started this work — the map "keeps your finger" — but left alone until the map moved to the top of the page, where a thumb now lands on it every time. **What was rejected:** deciding by direction, letting a vertical drag scroll and a horizontal one pan, which sounds clever and quietly makes it impossible to pan a map north or south — unacceptable on a map of river basins that run inland. **What was taken** is MapLibre's own `cooperativeGestures`, which is not a gesture of ours at all: switching it on hands the browser back `touch-action: pan-x pan-y`, so the page scrolls under one finger at the browser's own speed, with no JavaScript in the path, and two fingers move the map as they always did. A held-back drag says so for a moment, in Thai, Malay or English — never MapLibre's English default. The cost is real and is why it does not stand alone: **a map you cannot push with one finger is not a map you can read**, so the full-screen map, which has been the console's since 18 October, is now on the phone too, one tap from the map, with every gesture back and the page behind it still. It stops at the hotline bar rather than covering it (safety rule 4), Escape leaves it, and the three framing buttons — all four provinces, my places, reset — are in it. The handler is switched rather than set once, because the same map object is the console's, the page's and the full-screen one. — The fix for a map that eats scrolling is not a cleverer gesture; it is to stop guessing what the finger meant, and to give the map a place where there is nothing to guess.
- 2026-10-21 — **Approved: place checks, both halves** (owner: "checks OK"). The whole feature of 20 October stands as built, and the calls that were left open are settled by the approval. Teams: the round list at `/authority/places`, longest unchecked first, disputed places above it, two taps to answer. Residents: the ask on the pin and on the card that opens from it, never on the home screen's list. Every safe place carries when somebody was last there and who, and gains a grey "not checked since" marker past a window that tightens with the level, without ever losing the status it had. What residents say is an attributed line counting distinct people, and it reaches the covering team; where no team covers the tambon it becomes the status itself, marked as a resident's word. One person is enough there, and the provenance line is what makes that acceptable. A resident's "closed" keeps the place in the top three; an admin's or a team's still removes it. No check ever issues an alert or notifies anybody. Two things stay open and are not part of this approval: a page of the owner's own for disagreements, which today reach the covering team instead, and suggested corrections to the record itself. The printable shelter-door QR is parked, and costs no money - only a one-time sign-in by whoever scans it.
- 2026-10-21 — **A drill can be sent, to a chosen audience, in words that cannot be read as a real alert** (the owner: "when selecting Drill, should also have 2 choices more, drill no alert or drill with alert... this to show case that the system is working and we are now making sure it works", then "tailor in the messages sent, do not make it seems like real one... the admin is testing the alert system", then "add another selection to send 'Just rescue teams', of course these choices should be able to select multiple choices"). Until now a drill was two things at once: not a real alert, and invisible — which proves nothing about the road an alert travels down, days before a launch whose whole risk is that the road is untested. The two halves come apart. `is_drill` keeps its exact meaning and every rule written on it stands unchanged: a drill never suppresses the automated alarm, never gates a field rescue, is never counted in the usage figures, and **never moves `tambon_level`**, which is what the place-check windows and every other operational threshold read — pinned explicitly in this migration, because a drill is now readable and would otherwise have been picked up there. The new column `drill_audience` opens one road only, and the admin may tick several: the admins, the verified rescue teams, every verified unit, everyone in the chosen area. Empty is the silent drill, exactly as before. **Three things make it safe to send.** (1) **A sent drill never carries the template's instructions.** The database swaps them for a reviewed drill message — this is a drill, there is no hazard, you need do nothing, and in a real event this would have been a Warning for your tambon and would have told you what to do — because an instruction read out of its first line, or heard read aloud, is an instruction. The level's own "what to do" line is dropped from the card for the same reason; the level **badge** stays, since safety rule 3 does not let a level be hidden and the point of the drill is that the real one looks like this. (2) **It arrives exactly like the real thing**: same push urgency, same sound, same two channels, with the drill word first in the title, where a phone cuts a notification off. (3) **Who sees it is who it was sent to** — a drill to the teams is unreadable by a resident or a visitor, enforced by the read policy and not by any screen. Teams and admins are told **wherever they are**, by coverage rather than by their own saved places, since a team covering Tak Bai need not live there. Also added, useful beyond drills but mostly for them: **"everywhere people use Jaga"** as an area to tick, every tambon that is somebody's home or watched place, worked out on the server so the browser is never handed a map of where Jaga's users live. And a fact that was already true and is now held by a test: one person with a home and three watched places inside the alert is told **once**, by one push per device and one LINE message per account — for a drill and for a real alert alike. — A warning road that has never carried a message is not known to work, and the only thing standing between a convincing drill and a false alarm is what the drill says about itself.
- 2026-10-21 — **On a phone and a tablet the home screen reads alert, then what to do, then the background** (the owner: SOS, the flood report, the request for food and water and "what I have sent" should be "on top of Long-term flood risk, not today's alert box", and "only on the phone and tablet"). The page is written as three columns — what is happening, what to do, and the background — and below the laptop's breakpoint those columns stack, which put **every button after the whole of the first column**: the long-term risk card and the live measurements came before SOS. Measured at 360 x 640 before the change, SOS sat 2,400 px down; it is 1,544 px now, and the long-term risk has moved from 1,324 px to 2,185 px. **The alert keeps the top**: the owner asked for that in the same sentence, and the level of the reader's own tambon has been the first thing on the home screen since the first build. Done with `order` on blocks the page already writes — the three column wrappers become `display: contents` below `lg`, and three rules place what to do (1), the long-term risk and the measurements (2) and the reader's own places (3) — so **nothing is written twice**, unlike the dam card, and a screen reader meets the page in the order the eye does. The media query is the exact complement of Tailwind's `lg:` and asks nothing about the pointer, because that is where the two columns begin: a laptop and a tablet wide enough for two columns already have the buttons beside the alert, not under it. Two orders are left exactly as they were: **at Evacuate SOS stays directly under the hero** (decision 2026-09-29), and the checklist still comes before the buttons at Warning. — What to do is worth more than what to know, and on a phone it was three screens below it.
- 2026-10-21 — **Correction to the figures in the entry above** (the home screen's order on a phone). Two of the three numbers in it were worked out on paper from the heights of the blocks, not measured, and one was wrong. Measured at 360 x 640 by taking the new rules out of the live stylesheet and reading the page again: before the change **SOS sat at 2,464 px** (the entry says 2,400) **and the long-term risk card at 1,544 px** (the entry says 1,324); after it, SOS is at 1,544 px and the risk card at 2,185 px, which the entry has right. The change is the same and so is the reason; only the before-figures move, and they move in its favour. The probe is `.claude/local/q-homebefore.mjs`, which reads the page as built and then again with the rules deleted. — A measurement that was reasoned rather than taken should say so or be taken; this one said neither.
- 2026-10-21 — **An alert reaches the people who signed in and never said where they are** (the owner: "I want also for any users who logged in before but not selecting location getting the alert as well"). Jaga has been telling only accounts with a home tambon or a watched place. Measured on `jaga-dev` while answering this: **25 of 27 accounts have neither**, 23 of them LINE sign-ins — so a drill "to everyone in the area" would have reached two people, and on the day it matters the rest would have been told nothing at all. They are now told about **every alert, real or a sent drill**; the owner chose that over drills only, and the reasoning is that somebody who signs into an app for four provinces is probably in one of them, and a warning missed is worse than a warning read for a tambon you are not in. **What had to change with them is the words.** An alert's stored text names its real area, and the personal road replaces that with "your area" — true for somebody whose own tambon is in the alert, a lie to somebody who never named one. So these recipients are marked `placeless` by the target function and the sender gives them the stored words: "ต.ตะลุโบะ", not "พื้นที่ของคุณ". Push needed nothing for this, since its words already name the real area. Three limits kept deliberately. A drill sent to the admins or the teams does **not** reach them, because those audiences are by role and by coverage, not by place. The silent drill still tells nobody. And the moment somebody chooses a place they stop being in this set, so a Songkhla resident is not told about Yala — held by a test, as is the whole set. The review step now says how many of the people it is about to reach have no place of their own, because an admin should know that most of an alert's audience never chose one before that is a fact about a particular alert. — The people most likely to be missed by a warning system are the ones who did not finish setting it up.
- 2026-10-21 — **Three bands instead of two: the console's floor rises to 1,280 px, and a tablet held sideways gets a two-column console of its own** (the owner, after the measurements on the tracker's Tablet tab: "Go with B" and "floor OK"). Until today the console asked for 1,024 px and a pointer that can hover, and everything else got the phone's map-first page. Measured on the live site at six tablet sizes (`.claude/local/q-tablet.mjs`), that had two faults. **A tablet with a keyboard reports a fine pointer**, so it passed the test and was handed the three-column console at widths it was never measured at: the map came out **216×562** upright on an iPad Pro and **369×288** on an iPad sideways, with the readout strip wrapped to three rows — the smallest map Jaga served anywhere. And a tablet held sideways, which has the room for a dashboard, was given a phone's page. So: **laptop** is now 1,280 px and a hovering pointer; **tablet** is 1,024 px and up held sideways that is not a laptop (a finger, a keyboard, or a narrow window on a laptop); **phone** is everything else, every tablet held upright included — at 744–1,024 px two columns and a map worth looking at do not fit, and that arrangement already gives such a screen a 976×984 map. The tablet band is the same console with **two columns rather than three**: the readouts across the top, the map and the answer panel beside each other, and the left rail's controls in a full-width row beneath them, wrapping into columns instead of standing in a ladder. The arithmetic is why: rail 304 + panel 416 + gaps 40 is 760 px gone before the map. **Because a finger cannot hover**, a readout there is opened by a tap and stays open until tapped again — the pin is always visible and thumb-sized, the two cards that open nothing on the map carry a full-card toggle (`data-tile-toggle`, which is the pin under a bigger target and is on every screen), a stuck `:hover` from a tap is put back, and the folded panel's hover peek is switched off so only its button brings it back. — What a screen gets should follow what fits on it and what the hand can do, not one number.
- 2026-10-21 — **The admin ticks which roads an alert travels, so a drill need not spend the LINE quota** (the owner: "add option for admin to select send to Line or not. So for drill i don't need to lose Line quota"). Push costs nothing; LINE is metered against a monthly quota Jaga already tracks and warns about at 70% and 90%, and a drill to everyone would spend it on a message nobody needed. `alerts.channels` holds the choice, **default both for a real alert and a drill alike** — a drill that leaves LINE out is not a test of LINE, so that is a decision to make each time rather than a default. The choice is kept on the alert and enforced by the two target functions rather than only acted on by the server, so a retry, a later caller or a second console cannot quietly spend the quota the admin declined. **An alert with no road ticked is allowed and is not a mistake**: it stands in the app, on the map and in the bell exactly as it does today when no push key is configured, and the review step says so in those words. The quota warning disappears when LINE is unticked, because there is then nothing to spend. — The road that costs money should be a decision, not a default, and the decision belongs on the record rather than in the sender.
- 2026-10-21 — **A muted watched place is a choice to be left alone, not an absence of one** (fixing the same day's rule above it). `users_without_a_place()` asked for nobody with a *notifying* saved place, which put somebody who had saved a place and deliberately turned its notifications off into the same set as somebody who never chose anywhere — so muting a place quietly signed them up to every alert in all four provinces, the exact opposite of what they had asked for. The set is now what its name says: no home and **no saved place at all**. Found by the existing A7 LINE test, which had asserted since 14 October that a muted place drops its owner out of an alert's recipients and failed the moment the broader rule landed; it is the reason that test exists. — An old test earning its keep: the dangerous bugs in a warning system are the ones that add people to a list, because nothing about them looks broken.
- 2026-10-21 — **Jaga does not choose the sound a closed phone makes, so it chooses the shaking and rings the moment the app is opened** (owner, after sending themselves a drill: "my laptop has the lert voice but my phone and tablet didn't ring the sound. And both of them get the notification from the bell ring but no alert ring sound"). The laptop rang because a page was open: Jaga's alert sounds are made with the Web Audio API by the page itself (`lib/sounds.ts`), and the hook that plays them only exists while a window does. With Jaga closed there is no page, a service worker has no audio of its own, and the web gives no way to name a sound file for a notification — so the phone plays whatever it plays for Jaga, which is the person's own notification setting, and nothing Jaga ships can choose it, lengthen it or make it louder. That limit is now written where it will be read: in the doc-comment of `lib/use-alert-ring.ts` and in `app/sw.ts`. Two things were in reach and are now done. **The notification carries the level's own vibration** — the same pattern the sound carries in an open app, so an evacuation does not feel like a watch; Android honours it, iOS ignores it. **The tap on the notification is treated as the gesture it is**: the worker tells the window it was opened that way, the window arms the sound and rings the alert that brought the person there, instead of waiting for them to touch the screen a second time. A browser that does not count the tap refuses and the next touch does it, as before. The practical loud path on a closed phone remains LINE, which rings LINE's own notification and is why both roads are ticked by default for a real alert.
- 2026-10-21 — **The public map grades a river gauge by that gauge's own levels, and a gauge at or over its bank gets its own word** (owner, after spotting it: "one says in the Map as almost reach the river bank but on the Signals page doesn't seem to show the same"). Two gradings had drifted apart. `station_status()`, which the public map reads, graded on the `stations` columns: red at `warning_m` (the bank) and amber at `coalesce(watch_m, warning_m - 0.5)` — and `watch_m` is set on only 2 of the 48 gauges, so for every other one "close to its bank" was the flat guess of half a metre below it. The admins' signals meanwhile grade on `public.gauge_levels`, which holds a Watch, Warning and Evacuate level per gauge (from `thresholds gauges`, and from an admin through `admin_set_gauge_levels`). Measured on jaga-dev across the 46 gauges that have both: the map turned amber later than the real Watch on 34 of them (0.69 m late on average, 4.74 m at worst), red later than the real Warning on 39 and never earlier, and an admin's own levels never reached the public map at all. So `gauge_levels` is now what the public map reads (migration `..a10_station_status_gauge_levels`), with the `stations` columns kept as the fallback for a gauge with no row there; the function is `security definer` because `gauge_levels` is admin-read-only, and no level value leaves it — only the status word, and the bank level that was public before. Because Evacuate equals the bank on all 46 gauges and Warning sits below it on all 46, there are three live bands where the map drew two, so the map gained a fourth status, `above_evacuate`, rather than folding it into `above_warning`: a level is never shown lower than it is (safety rule 3). The three bands now read "above its normal level", "close to its bank" and "at or over its bank", a gauge mark gained a sixth look (`high`, a glass just over half full, no ripple), and the public wording still avoids the words Watch, Warning and Evacuate, because a gauge reading is a measurement and never an alert (safety rule 2). The monitoring card calls a river an event from the Warning band up: above its normal level is counted and reported, not announced.
- 2026-10-21 — **An evacuation knocks three times on a closed phone, a warning twice, and the two lower levels once** (owner, after the sound fix: "the sound alerts only when I open the app on my phone... Can we make it ring even before app openning? at least for devices that added the app to Homescreen?"; the levels chosen by them through AskUserQuestion). Adding Jaga to the home screen does not let it ring: a sound is made by an open page, a service worker has no audio, and the web has no way to put a sound on a notification — the limit is the platform's and no install changes it. What a worker *can* do is show the notification again, which makes the phone sound and shake again. So `KNOCKS` in `lib/push.ts` gives **evacuate** two further knocks at 8s and 20s and **warning** one at 12s, while **watch** and **safe to return** stay at one. The reasoning for stopping there: a notification that nags at 2am is how people come to turn Jaga off, and an alert nobody is subscribed to in November is worth less than one they hear once in October. A drill knocks exactly as the real alert of its level would, pinned by a test, because a quieter drill would stop being a test of the thing it is drilling. The worker stops knocking the moment the person dismisses the notification or has Jaga visible in front of them, so it never talks over the app's own sound. The one thing an install genuinely buys is on Android, where an installed Jaga gets its own entry in the phone's notification settings and its sound and importance can be set; a new per-platform guide (`components/push/SoundGuide.tsx`, `push.sound` in the three message files) says so and says plainly, on the face of it, that Jaga cannot do it for them. On an iPhone the standard notification sound is all there is, so those steps are about the ring switch, the volume and Focus instead.

- 2026-10-21 — **A reservoir gets the same rain cloud a person's own place gets** (owner: "add cloud animation also when there is raining on each Reservoirs ... so we can see that the water increment in the reservoir due to the heavy rain in the reservoir area"). The clouds built on 15 Oct over a home and watched places now also stand over every dam's lake, placed at the middle of the reservoir's box (`reservoirCentre`, `damRainPlaces` in `lib/dam-map.ts`) with no lift, so the drops fall onto the water rather than above a pin. They are drawn whether the person's own pins are shown or not, because the rain over a lake is not about where that person lives. Three things were held to deliberately: the cloud says only what is falling now, from the same Open-Meteo reading the weather page uses - it never says what the lake will do, and the pop-up and the card point at the dam's own figures for that (safety rules 2 and 8); no cloud appears unless rain is falling there, as before, so a cloud over a reservoir still means something; and the person's own places are asked for first, so a long list of watched places never costs them a cloud of their own (`MAX_POINTS` raised from 12 to 20 for the six dams). — Rain over the catchment is the cause of a rise behind the wall, and the map was showing the rain only where people live.

- 2026-10-21 — **The map's own box now clips its HTML markers** (found while building the reservoir clouds above). The map container carried `overflow-hidden` but was `position: static`, and an `overflow` clip on a static box does not apply to an absolutely positioned child, whose containing block is the wrapper outside it. MapLibre sets `position: relative` on the container itself, but React owns that element's class attribute and writes it again on the next render, so the library's class is lost. The result: a marker whose point is off the edge of the frame was painted over the rest of the page - measured, three of the six reservoir clouds were drawn over the header and the layer panel. The fix is the word `relative` in the container's classes. It was invisible until now only because a person's own places are nearly always inside the frame they are looking at. — A library's own stylesheet cannot be relied on for an element React re-renders.

- 2026-10-21 — **The map on a tablet is laid out to Apple's Human Interface Guidelines** (the owner: research Apple's guidance and apply it to the tablet map; they chose "compact band + one-line readouts" over a full map-first reversal, and asked for all four of the smaller fixes alongside it). The guidance was read from Apple's own pages (Layout, Designing for iPadOS, Split views, the design tips) and applied against measurements of the live site at 1,180x820 with a finger, not against an impression: the map began **496 px down an 820 px window** - header 107, weather bar 247, readouts 126 - so a tablet opened on the chrome, which is the opposite of "people see primary content without scrolling" and of iPadOS's "elevate the content people care about". It now begins at 397 px and ends where the sticky hotline bar does, so **the whole map and the whole answer panel are on the screen the page opens on**. Nothing was removed to get there ("keep functionality the same as size classes change"): the head holds one line, the four hazards that are not live stand behind the disclosure a phone has had since 16 Oct, and a readout is as tall as what it says rather than carrying a laptop's 5rem floor. Four smaller things went with it. **The four map tools came off the map** - they floated over the visible top edge of it, against "position onscreen controls where they are easy to reach, but not in the way"; the frame keeps a 56 px strip for them above the canvas, which costs real map height and is the price of an unobstructed one. **Eleven controls under 44x44 pt were sized up** - the readout pins were 26 px wide, MapLibre's zoom buttons 29, the attribution toggle 24, the layer tick boxes 24 (a tick box is 28 px inside a 44 px line, which is what Apple's "the visible control may be smaller" allows). **No heading is cut**: the laptop shows a readout's heading in full on hover, and a finger cannot hover, so on a tablet the fourth and fifth read "รายงานจากประช..." and "ผล..." for good; the clamp is gone and the one readout with a control in its heading row gets a wider column rather than squeezing its heading. **The answer panel has a scroll edge**, a fade at its foot with matching room beneath its last block, so what fades is empty space at the end and real content only while there is more below - no scroll listener and nothing that needs scroll-driven animation. The cost of the pass is the map canvas at 1,180 px: 356 px tall rather than 468, all of it visible and none of it under a toolbar, with full screen one tap away. — The owner asked for Apple's philosophy applied, and Apple's first rule for a large display is that the content people came for is on the screen when the page opens.
- 2026-10-21 — Jaga is made findable by search: robots.txt, a sitemap of the eleven public pages in all three languages, canonical and hreflang on every public page, noindex on the consoles and the account pages, and schema.org on the home page naming the four provinces as the area served. — Measured on 21 Oct the live site had no robots.txt and no sitemap, carried no canonical or hreflang, and did not appear in a search for its own name; the flood peak is November to December and a non-profit with no advertising budget has no other way to be found.
- 2026-10-21 — The target is the local searches, not "flood situation in Thailand": the Thai and Malay queries that name one of the four covered provinces, plus Jaga's own name. — Ranking nationally would send people in provinces Jaga does not cover to an app that cannot warn them (safety rule 10), and ThaiWater, ปภ., กรมอุตุนิยมวิทยา and Thai PBS hold the national queries on authority Jaga will not have this season.
- 2026-10-21 — The home page title adds one word, "น้ำท่วม": "Jaga – เตือนภัยน้ำท่วมและขอความช่วยเหลือ" (and the Malay and English to match). — The word people actually search was in the description but not in the title, which is the strongest signal a page has; one word gets it in without turning the title into a keyword list. The installed app's name on a phone is `short_name`, still "Jaga", so nothing on a home screen changes.
- 2026-10-21 — Each covered province gets its own public page at /flood/<province>, rendered on the server in all three languages, with an English slug. — The map is drawn in the browser, so no page on the site carried a province in its text for a crawler to read; and a person who arrives on it cold needs the level in force, what the rivers read and the hotlines without waiting for JavaScript. An English slug keeps one address working in all three languages.
- 2026-10-21 — A public function that only aggregates runs as the caller (security invoker), not as the definer, unless it must read something the caller cannot. — province_public_summary was written security definer, which bypasses row-level security, so its safe-place count included places an admin had rejected while the public map excluded them. Running as the caller makes a public count equal what the public map shows by construction rather than by a promise in a comment.
- 2026-10-21 — Google Search Console is verified by a DNS TXT record at Cloudflare, not by a file or meta tag in the repository. — It verifies the whole domain including tiles.jagaapp.org, it survives redeploys and env changes, and it keeps a verification token out of a public repository.
- 2026-10-22 — **Everything waiting for an admin is in the admin's bell, each line a tap from the page that clears it, and none of them makes a sound** (owner: "make all that need admins attention goes to Admin's notification bell (Admin only). Like Pending Verify unit, or Any attention in Signals or pending anything that need Admin approval or review"; and, a minute later, "Once click at those notification line it should bring to the concerned page or box to let admin review"). The admin home has counted pending teams, field rescues and reports on its menu since it was built, but the menu is only seen by somebody already at the console. The bell is what an admin carries around, and it knew about two of these: the automatic alarms and the field rescues. So a team could sit unverified for a day because nobody happened to open the right page. `admin_attention()` now answers for seven queues in one call — teams waiting to be verified, flood reports waiting to be moderated, an unreviewed dam notice, SOS flagged as suspected spam, possible duplicate cases, rescues a team says it finished that nobody has confirmed, and alerts in force past their next-update time. Each line carries the queue's own words, its count and a link to where it is cleared. The shape is copied from `admin_field_waiting()` rather than invented: a count plus the newest record's id and time, where the id is what the bell remembers as read — so a queue goes quiet once seen and speaks again only when something newer joins it, stays while anything waits, and disappears when the queue is empty. It is not held to the bell's fourteen-day window, because a team that registered a month ago is still waiting. **None of them rings or is pushed** (the owner's choice, through AskUserQuestion): a backlog has no moment at which it happens, and a sound at 2am for a flood report is how an admin comes to mute the bell before the season. That needed an explicit exclusion in `ringForAll`, whose fallback would otherwise have given every new kind the quiet "notice" tone. The pending automatic alarm keeps its own sound, because that one publishes itself if nobody acts — rule 2's one exception, unchanged. The seventh queue, stale alerts, is the only line that is not an approval queue: it is there because the public is already being shown a grey "not updated since" marker (safety rule 3) and nothing told the admin it had happened. Admins only by `admin_only()`, and nothing personal crosses the boundary: a queue name, a count, an id and a time, pinned by an RLS test.

- 2026-10-22 — **The map's layer switches are in one place: "what the satellite saw" moves to the left rail, and the duplicate "Show safe places" goes** (the owner: "no need to have Show safe places again on the right panel, it's already on the left layers panel. Also bring the Show flood seen by satellite (GISTDA) to the left"). Where the console has two rails - a laptop, and a tablet held sideways - a reader could see **the same safe-places switch twice**: the chip in the left rail's "choose what to see on the map", and a switch of its own in the right-hand panel. They were never two things; both set the same `showPlaces`. The chip is the one that stays, and the console's copy is `display: none`, which takes the duplicate out of the tab order and out of what a screen reader reads as well - a keyboard met the same switch twice too. What it explained is not a duplicate and stays: the legend for the two pin shapes, a square with a house for people and a square with a P for cars. A phone has one column and meets them one after another, so it is left alone. **GISTDA's layer moved to the left rail, above the chips rather than below them, and that position was measured**: put after them it sat at y=960 on a 900 px laptop, inside a rail that is 324 px tall against 811 px of content - off the bottom of the window, in a column the reader would have to scroll to find it in. It was the first thing in the right-hand panel before and it is the first thing in this one now. **Everything the switch does is unchanged** - the risk classes come off, the map travels to the hexagons wherever in the four provinces they are, they blink three times, and closing it puts the frame and the classes back - with one thing added that the move made necessary: on a tablet the rail is a full-width row **under** the map, so the reader is now brought to the map when the answer arrives. Without it the map would travel and blink somewhere they could not see, which is the very thing the travelling was added to prevent. The same was already true on a phone, where the switch sits among the explainers under the map, and it is fixed there by the same rule; on a laptop nothing scrolls, because there the rail is the left-hand column and the map is beside it. Two lists had to learn the move: the full-screen map keeps its rail children by name, so `[data-satellite]` is named there now - and it was an end-to-end test, not a unit test, that caught it. — The owner asked for one switch per thing and for the satellite layer on the left; the measured position and the scroll-to-the-map are what it takes for that to work on a tablet.

- 2026-10-22 — **A report hexagon on the public map shows only once 2 different people have a standing report in it** (owner: "hexagons from 2", answering the A10 security review). Setting `report_hex.min_people`, never below 2; the per-tambon list is unchanged. — In the countryside one person's hexagon nearly points at their house (safety rule 6).
