- 2026-11-10 (later the same day) — **A19 applied to jaga-dev, and A12's dam-operator pair with it, in timestamp order.** The owner said "apply A19"; the order was the thing to settle first, because A12 part 2 (`20261109120100`, pending since 9 Nov) replaces the same four gate functions with the older `unit_is_dam_only` versions. Applied after A19 it would have silently reverted the planning lockdown - no error, no sign, safety rule 5 quietly undone. The owner chose timestamp order, which is what a fresh database and CI do. Applied by hand with `.claude/local/apply-migration.mjs`, each with its ledger row in the same transaction: `20261109120000_a12_dam_operator_type`, `20261109120100_a12_dam_operator`, `20261110180000_a19_planning_not_a_field_team`, `20261110180100_a19_highway_paths`. Then `uv run python -m highway_paths load --yes`: 4,366 sections and 197 stretches, 143 of them listed, replacing nothing and leaving no note behind (there were none to leave). Still pending on jaga-dev and deliberately untouched: `20261108090000_a16_weather_advisory` and the other session's `20261110170000_s11_gauge_week_trend`.

  **Verified after, against the applied schema:** all four gates call `unit_is_office_only` and none calls `unit_is_dam_only`, so A19 won the ordering; the capability test answers correctly for all nine combinations; the three tables exist with RLS on and one policy each, addressed to `authenticated` only; no policy admits anon or public, and reading all three as anon returns zero rows; `authenticated` holds no write grant; `drill_visible()` no longer asks the field-team gate. 34 checks, `.claude/local/a19-live-check.mjs`. As an admin the four boards read Pattani 32 listed stretches, Narathiwat 48, Songkhla 43, Yala 20; the map hands Pattani 749 features; the Yala export 597 rows with their names and geometry and none of the pipeline's prose. The alert count did not move (10). `tests/rls/dam-operator.test.ts` **37/37 against jaga-dev**, which is the first time A12 part 2 has been tested against a real applied schema at all - until today it could not be, because it uses the enum label its own part 1 adds.

  **Nobody's access changed today.** All five units on jaga-dev that hold `planning` also hold `rescue` and `coordination`, so every one of them is a team by the new test and keeps exactly what it had. The lockdown binds the next office to register, which is the one it was written for.

  **One defect found by reading the applied answer rather than the code**, fixed in `20261110213000_a19_planning_provinces_active`: `my_planning_provinces()` offered an admin all 77 Thai provinces, because `provinces` holds every one of them on purpose (safety rule 10: tapping an uncovered province is answered, not blank). 73 of those buttons led to an empty list. Now filtered to `status = 'active'`, which is exactly Songkhla, Pattani, Yala and Narathiwat.

  **And one test defect the real rows exposed.** Five of the 26 A19 policy tests asserted row *counts* - written when the table was empty, they broke the moment 4,366 real sections were in it. They now assert the property instead: every row this caller can see belongs to their province, and their own fixtures are among them. A policy test that can only pass on an empty table cannot be run against the database the live site reads, which is the only place it matters.

  Not done, by the owner's instruction: the Thai wording has not gone to their reviewer and the Malay has not gone to a Patani Malay reader. Both stay open. Also not done: the page has not been opened in a browser as a signed-in planning office - every database read behind it is verified, and the owner can see the page itself at `/authority/highway-paths`, which an admin may open for all four provinces.
- 2026-11-18 — **TMD's own warnings are quoted on Jaga's weather screens** (owner: can we also use TMD's free data for the weather widget and weather-related screens; yes). The Thai Meteorological Department is the national forecaster, and its "ประกาศเตือน" is what the television and the provincial offices read out. Until today Jaga's weather came only from Open-Meteo, and TMD appeared only as a telephone number (1182) and as the runbook's fallback. Now `/api/public/tmd/warnings` reads TMD's free `WeatherWarningNews` feed (no account needed) and the current notices for the South are quoted on the home screen, under the weather card, and at the top of the weather page.

  **Why TMD and not just a second model:** a resident who hears a national warning on the radio and then opens Jaga should not have to work out which of the two is current. Showing TMD's notice inside Jaga, with TMD's name on it, answers that.

  **How it stays clearly not a Jaga alert** (safety rules 2, 3, 8 and 10): the card carries no Jaga level, no alert colour and no all-clear - brand slate and teal only, and a unit test fails if any alert token appears in it. It names TMD as the issuer, the time TMD announced it and the period it covers, and links TMD's own PDF. It says in the reader's own language that this is TMD's announcement and not a Jaga alert. Nothing in this path can publish, raise or lower a Jaga alert; the one automatic publishing path remains the gauge alarm, which reads gauges and not forecasts. On a day the reader's own tambon is at Warning or Evacuate the card is **not drawn at all**, under the same condition as the weather card: then the screen belongs to Jaga's own alert, and a second warning voice beside it would only raise the question of which to follow.

  **TMD writes in Thai only, so the notice is quoted in Thai on every locale**, marked `lang="th"`, with our own line in the reader's language saying that TMD announces in Thai only. Safety text is never machine-translated at runtime; the honest thing on the Malay and English screens is our words around TMD's quote, plus TMD's number for anyone who needs it explained.

  **Relevance is read from the prose, because the feed carries no area codes at all** - only Thai text. A notice is kept if it names the South, the Gulf of Thailand or one of the four provinces, or if it is a storm notice (announced for the whole country). The test is deliberately generous: a resident who reads a warning for the next province along has lost nothing, and TMD's own text always names the area it is about. A notice about the North alone is dropped. Expired notices go six hours after the period they cover ends, and anything TMD left no time on at all is dropped rather than shown as current.

  **The feed is somebody else's text arriving over the network and ending up on a safety screen**, so: a hand reader rather than a new XML dependency (budget rule); junk, an empty feed or a truncated record yields no warnings instead of throwing; TMD's Bangkok timestamps are read as +07:00, which a seven-hour mistake would turn into a warning announced in the future; and only an https link on tmd.go.th is passed into an `href`. The request goes through our own server and is cached at the edge for ten minutes, with `stale-if-error` running six hours - during a storm the government feed is exactly what is under load, and yesterday evening's notice with its time on it beats an empty box. TMD unreachable looks like a quiet day, and touches nothing else on the screen.

  Verified: 27 unit tests on the reading and filtering, 9 on what the card says, lint and typecheck clean on every file touched, and the live route checked against the real feed - it returned TMD's notice 8 of 18 Nov (heavy rain over the lower South, 18-22 Nov) with its times, its period and its PDF.

  **Still the owner's:** writing to TMD to confirm in writing that the warnings may be quoted in a public non-profit app, and whether Jaga should have its own `uid`/`ukey` rather than the pair TMD prints in its public example (`TMD_API_UID`/`TMD_API_KEY` in `.env.example` are ready and empty); the new Thai lines to their reviewer and the Malay to a Patani Malay reader. Registration for TMD's 3 km forecast model (data.tmd.go.th/nwpapi/register) asks for personal details, so it waits for the owner too.
- 2026-11-18 — **A situation report a team or an agency can print, file and forward** (`/authority/sitrep`; owner: "add SITREP export for agencies, teams"). Jaga's consoles are live screens; a SITREP is the opposite — one page, frozen at a stated moment, for somebody who was not looking at the screen. That is what gets read out at the 08:00 meeting and attached to the memo asking for boats.

  **Four choices the owner made, and the reasons they matter:**

  *Verified units only, not admins.* Admins already have `/admin/exports`. The report is scoped to one unit's own coverage and gated in the database (`unit_sitrep`), open to the registrant and active dispatchers. A field member gets none (they read only their own assigned case), and an **office-only unit — a dam operator or a planning office — gets none either**, because it is not a team in the field and holds no cases (safety rule 5, the `unit_is_office_only` test from A19).

  *Counts only.* No point, phone, name, requester's words, photo, or team position. This was the decision everything else hangs on: a SITREP is **forwarded by design** — to the district office, into a group chat, onto a projector — so anything personal in it would travel exactly that far. Nothing in the report is restricted data, which is why it can be handed to an agency at all (safety rules 5 and 6).

  *Both formats.* The printed page is the PDF: the browser's own print dialogue has "Save as PDF" on every phone and laptop, so there is no PDF renderer and no second set of wording to keep reviewed. Word (.docx) was added because a Thai district or provincial office works in Word — a report they can paste their own heading onto gets used; a PDF gets retyped. Plus a CSV of the per-tambon table. One new dependency, `docx` (MIT, free, server-side only); every string in the Word file comes from `messages/*.json` through the route, so the document and the page cannot drift apart.

  *The 06:00 / 18:00 Bangkok cut-off*, not a rolling 24 hours. Two units reporting the same flood have to be reporting the same hours or the district office cannot add their numbers up; a rolling window makes every copy incomparable with every other. A hand-chosen period is allowed up to seven days and is labelled as chosen by hand.

  **Safety rules kept, explicitly:** it is not an alert and cannot raise a level, prefill a draft or notify anybody (rule 2); an alert past its next-update time keeps its level and gains the "not updated since" marker, in print as on screen (rule 3); the four limits print with the report in every format, in the body and never as an appendix, and each river reading carries its own observation time (rule 8); the hotlines are in the document, because it is read where there is no app (rule 4); every generation is in `audit_log` as `read_sitrep` (rule 5). No alert colour is used anywhere in it — in print the level is a word on white.

  Verified: 18 unit tests (the cut-off arithmetic, including the same instant named in two time zones; the document id; the table order; the three languages' keys; all four limits present in each; a real .docx built and checked), lint, typecheck and `npm run build` all clean.

  **Not verified, and the owner's to allow:** `supabase/migrations/20261118120000_sitrep.sql` has never been executed, and `tests/rls/sitrep.test.ts` (7 cases: the counts, merged cases excluded, a dispatcher yes and a field member no, four kinds of outsider refused, the period limits, the audit row, a drill never shown) has never been run. The only Postgres on this laptop is `jaga-dev`, which is the database the live site reads, so running even a rolled-back transaction against it needs the owner's yes.
- 2026-11-18 (same day) — **Rain actually measured, beside the forecast: TMD's station readings on the weather page.** The second half of the owner's "use TMD's free data" ask. Everything else on Jaga's weather screens is a model's opinion about the future; this is a reading of the past - the 24-hour rainfall total at TMD's nearest stations, each with its distance and the hour it was read. During a flood that is the number people actually argue about.

  **Only the 24-hour total is shown.** The feed's other rainfall field carries no stated accumulation period, and a figure whose period we would be guessing at has no place on a safety screen.

  **Two limits the card states rather than hides.** TMD has about seven stations across four provinces, so the nearest can be tens of kilometres away: the card prints the distance and says in the reader's own language that this is the station's figure and not their village's, because rain here differs greatly within a few kilometres (safety rule 8). And **no station further than 80 km is offered at all.** That came out of the live feed, not from tidiness: each round carries only the stations that have reported, and on the evening of 18 Nov one round held all seven while the next held two, both in Songkhla. Without the limit a reader in Narathiwat would have been shown Sa Dao's rain from 190 km away, looking exactly like their own. Beyond ~80 km the card says nothing instead.

  **A bug the tests caught, worth recording because the shape of it recurs:** `Number("")` is 0 in JavaScript, so an empty field would have been published as "0 mm fell" - a station that sent no reading reported as a station that measured no rain. On a flood screen those are opposite statements (the Thai style guide's rule that "ไม่พบ" must never become "ไม่มี", in code). `toNumber` now returns null for an empty field, a measured 0 is still shown as 0, and a missing one as a dash; both cases have a test.

  Verified: 24 unit tests on the reading, filtering and distance, 8 on what the card says, 5 new e2e cases in a real browser at 360 px (TMD's warning quoted as TMD's and above the forecast, no card on a quiet day, the station figures with a measured zero, and the forecast unaffected when TMD is unreachable) - the whole weather spec 17/17, home 28/29 with the one pre-existing coming-soon failure. The live route was checked against the real feed twice, which is how the 80 km rule was found.
- 2026-11-18 (same day, third part) — **TMD's own 3 km model shown beside Open-Meteo's, so no single number is presented as certain.** The third part of the owner's TMD ask, and the one that needed a design choice rather than a reader.

  **Why two models rather than a better one.** Safety rule 8 says never present a forecast as certain, and the most honest way to show that is two independent forecasts of the same rain. Where they agree, a reader can lean on the figure; where they disagree - 20 mm against 60 mm for the same night - the disagreement *is* the forecast, and one tidy number would be a lie of omission. TMD also runs at 3 km over Thailand against Open-Meteo's global grid, so behind the hills of Yala and Narathiwat it has detail the global model cannot have.

  **What the card does with a disagreement:** names it, and points at the **higher** figure. On a flood app the cost of being ready for rain that does not come is not the cost of being caught by rain that does. The bands are coarse on purpose (under 5 mm both models are describing a dry day; a 10 mm or 50% gap is "agree"; past three times and 30 mm it is "far apart"), because a percentage between 2 mm and 6 mm means nothing to anyone. The card carries no level and no alert colour, and a test fails if an alert token appears in it. Nothing in this path can publish or feed the automated alarm, which reads gauges and not forecasts.

  **Dark until the owner registers.** TMD's model API is free but needs an account, and registering asks for personal details, so only the owner can do it (data.tmd.go.th/nwpapi/register, then "Create New Token" - shown once, not stored by TMD). Without `TMD_NWP_TOKEN` the route answers 503 like any unavailable source and the card is simply not drawn: the weather screens are byte for byte what they were, with no gap, no error and no mention of TMD. An e2e test asserts exactly that state, because it is the live site's state today.

  **Unverified against the live API, and said so in the code.** Written from TMD's published documentation and never run against the real endpoint, because no token exists. The reader is therefore forgiving: it accepts either spelling of the top-level key (TMD's own docs show `WeatherForcasts` in one place and `WeatherForecast` in another), ignores unknown fields, skips an hour with an unreadable time without losing the rest, and returns null rather than a half-built forecast for anything else - 10 malformed bodies are tested, including an error page served as JSON. TMD's 12 condition codes are mapped onto the app's existing weather groups, and a test asserts every mapped group is one the app already has words and an icon for in three languages. **The first job once a token exists is one real request checked against `tests/unit/tmd-forecast.test.ts`.**

  Verified: 17 unit tests on the request, the reader and the agreement bands (including symmetry both ways round), 8 on what the card says, 2 new e2e cases - the gap named with both figures, and the page unchanged without a token. Rate limits are 60 requests a minute and 100,000 data points an hour; the point is rounded to ~1 km and the answer cached at the edge for 30 minutes, so the whole service area costs a few dozen requests an hour however many people are reading.
- 2026-11-18 (same day, fourth part) — **One live signal for residents, in plain language: a river that is rising, and rain in the next three hours.** The owner's ask: "one sentence, not a data panel", and the reason was trust rather than data - if the television or Naraflood is talking about heavy rain while Jaga still shows a calm green ปกติ, people decide Jaga is not watching.

  **Most of the shape already existed** (the monitoring card, 17 Oct) and was left alone: one line, a small moving mark, the detail behind a tap, "these are measurements, not an alert" underneath. What it could not say were exactly the owner's two sentences, and both gaps were real.

  **"The river near you is rising."** `station_status()` has measured each gauge's three-hour rise and its seven-day trend all along, and the map console reads both - but the home screen's smaller feed projected them away, keeping only position, status and time. So the card could say a river was *near its bank* and never that one was *climbing*, which is the earlier and more useful sentence. Both numbers now ride along on the same database read (a few hundred bytes a station, no new query, no new cron). A rise is counted on the same 5 cm in three hours the map already draws a chevron for, so the sentence and the arrows it sends a reader to can never disagree, and across the reader's own areas, like the two bank lines above it. A rising river below its bank now moves the card from "all readings within their normal range" to naming what is moving. It is still not a level and suggests none (safety rule 2); the seven-day trend stays an aside, because it is the season's background and not an event.

  **"Heavy rain is expected in the next three hours."** The rain line read the hour in progress and said "มีฝนตกตามแบบจำลอง" - model-speak, and about now rather than next. The header's weather chip already keeps 24 hourly rows on the device, so looking three hours ahead costs no bytes and makes no request. The case that matters is a dry hour with a wet night in the model: the card used to tell that reader there was no rain, which is precisely the credibility gap the owner described.

  **Where the word "heavy" comes from.** Open-Meteo's own condition code for those hours, never a millimetre threshold of ours. A threshold would have been Jaga asserting a severity nobody measured, and TMD's "ฝนตกหนัก" is defined over 24 hours, not three. Thirty millimetres under a light-rain code is therefore not called heavy, and a test says so.

  **"No flood alert yet" was deliberately not put in the line.** It stays the standing note under the card, with the hero's level badge a few inches above carrying the other half. A single line that says both "rain expected" and "no alert yet" starts to read as an all-clear, which is what safety rule 10 exists to prevent.

  **A bug the tests caught:** the three-hour total was rounded to one decimal *before* being compared with the dry threshold, so 0.05 mm became 0.1 mm and crossed it - the display deciding whether rain was forecast at all. The threshold now reads the unrounded sum; the rounded figure is only for the words.

  Nothing here publishes, raises a level or feeds the automated alarm, which reads gauges and not forecasts.

  Verified: 28 new unit tests (12 on the forecast helper, 6 on the rising count, 10 on what the card says), lint, typecheck and `npm run build` clean, and the six new message lines formatted in all three languages. **The unit runner itself is broken on this laptop** - vitest 5.0.2 fails to start a suite ("Vitest failed to find the current suite") on every file, including ones untouched by this work, so these tests were verified by compiling the same assertions with esbuild and running them under node, and CI is the real check.

## 2026-11-18 - The home page on a laptop reads like a website, not three columns

**Decision.** The owner, looking at the laptop home screen: "rearrange the home page elements to
be more like a website, instead of 3 vertical columns like the current one." Offered three shapes,
they chose the banded one, and chose a single narrow column at Warning or Evacuate over keeping
the bands.

**Why the three columns of 16 Oct had to go.** Three even columns of stacked cards is what an app
looks like, not a page. The alert - the one thing the screen exists for - was a third of the width
with nothing above it, and the three stacks ended at wildly different heights.

**What it is now, at 1,280 px and wider (and on a tablet held sideways):**

1. the alert for their own tambon, two thirds of the width, with the rail beside it carrying
   everything that is theirs - SOS, a flood report, food and water, what they have sent, and the
   places they watch;
2. under the alert, in the same wide stack, what is happening: the weather, TMD's warning, the
   neighbours, the rivers, the long-term risk, the reservoir;
3. the way into the map, full width, as a row of seven tiles rather than a narrow two-abreast
   stack;
4. a foot of two: the safe places, and "share Jaga".

**At Warning or Evacuate it is one column, capped at 46rem and centred** (safety rule 3: nothing
sits beside an alert). That column is the phone's own approved order with one difference the owner
asked for - **SOS directly under the alert, not after the checklist.** On a phone the whole screen
is one thumb's reach; a laptop window is not, and measured at 1,440 px the button had been a
thousand pixels down. The reservoir's drawing, which a laptop writes in the wide stack (16 Oct),
is pushed to the end there for the same reason: it had put nearly 900 px between the checklist and
SOS.

**How, without touching what a phone reads.** The three columns of 8 Oct are still what is
written. Inside them the blocks are grouped into named bands (`data-home-band` in
components/home/HomeView.tsx) which are `display: contents` everywhere except the console, so a
phone, a tablet held upright and a narrow window still read the page as the one run of blocks they
always did. The one block that moved in the markup is the person's own places, from the top of the
third column to the foot of the rail - it still reads with the places group, because the phone's
`order` rules now name the bands rather than the columns, and because the rail is written
immediately before that column the two-column layout at 1,024-1,280 px portrait is unchanged as
well.

**One thing tried and rejected:** cutting both columns into bands by grid row, so that the alert
and the SOS button shared row 1. A grid row is as tall as the tallest thing in it, so a short
alert beside a tall rail left 650 px of white under the alert (measured at 1,440 px). Two whole
stacks have no row to share.

Verified in a real browser at five widths and three states (360 phone, 1,024 portrait, 1,100
landscape, 1,440 laptop normal and at Warning): the order of blocks is unchanged everywhere except
the laptop, and no screen scrolls sideways. Unit tests for the arrangement in
tests/unit/phone-map.test.tsx; lint, typecheck and the touched suites clean.

**Also found:** vitest is not broken on this laptop - it fails only when run from Git Bash
("Vitest failed to find the current suite" on every file, including untouched ones). The same
command from PowerShell runs the whole suite. Noted in CLAUDE.local.md.

## 2026-11-19 - TMD's forecast model verified against the live API, and a layout bug it exposed

The owner registered at data.tmd.go.th/nwpapi and supplied `TMD_NWP_TOKEN` (issued 19 Nov 2026,
expires **19 Nov 2027** - renewal is on the tracker's Reminders tab and in `docs/runbook.md`).

**The reader was written blind on 18 Nov and is now checked.** One request per point at Pattani,
Yala, Narathiwat and Hat Yai. Everything the reader assumed held, with three things worth writing
down: the live top-level key is `WeatherForecasts` (TMD's own documentation also shows
`WeatherForcasts` and `WeatherForecast`, and all three are still accepted, because a feed that
disagrees with itself in writing may disagree with itself in production); `location` comes back as
TMD's **grid point**, about 1 km from the point asked for, so the forecast reports the position it
is actually for and not the one requested; and `duration=24` gives 24 contiguous hourly entries
from the current Bangkok hour, each stamped `+07:00`, with rain, temperature and a condition code
on every one. Condition codes 2-6 were seen live and all map to groups the app already draws. The
first five hours of the real Pattani answer are now a verbatim fixture in
`tests/unit/tmd-forecast.test.ts`: if TMD changes the shape of its answer, that test goes red.

**The comparison earns its place on the evidence of its first day.** Over the 24 h from 08:00 on
19 Nov, Open-Meteo and TMD said: Pattani 8.2 against 41.7 mm, Narathiwat 8.7 against 59.8 mm, Yala
9.7 against 30 mm, Hat Yai 10.9 against 8.7 mm. Three of the four points disagree, two of them
far apart, and TMD is the wetter in all three - which is also the direction of TMD's own warning
for the lower South. A single figure on that page would have been Open-Meteo's ~9 mm, and would
have read as settled. The card names the gap and points at the higher number (safety rule 8).

**A real bug found by a test that had never been able to run.** The weather page's laptop grid was
given TMD's two cards inside `lg:col-span-2` wrappers. A wrapper is a child of a `gap-5` column
whether or not the card inside it draws anything, and TMD's cards draw nothing in the ordinary
case - no token, no station within 80 km, or TMD unreachable. So the page carried about 40 px of
dead space at its foot on every phone, which is the common case, not the exception. Fixed by
removing the wrappers: the cards are grid children in their own right and wear the width
themselves, through a `className` prop and `FULL_WIDTH` exported from `WeatherView` beside the
grid it belongs to. The layout test now asserts the invariant it was written for - an even number
of half-width cards - rather than a frozen count of five, so a card that comes and goes with its
data cannot break the pairing unnoticed.

This was shipped on 18 Nov believing the unit runner was broken. It was not: `vitest run` fails
per-file on this laptop by timing out while spawning a worker for each file, and `--no-isolate`
reuses one worker and runs the whole suite - 2,032 tests in 72 s. That is now the way to run it
here, and is how this bug surfaced. The lesson is the plain one: a test suite believed broken is a
test suite not running, and the cost lands on whatever shipped meanwhile.

## 2026-11-19 - The reservoir's drawing moves into the rail, and four stale unit tests

**The owner, seeing the new laptop home page:** "I want it filled the empty place with reservoir
card as you suggest." On an ordinary day the rail (SOS, a flood report, food and water, what they
have sent, the places they watch) ran out around 800 px while the wide column beside it ran on to
about 2,800, so the lower right of the page was empty. The reservoir's drawing is the one block
long enough to fill it and quiet enough to belong there - it is background, not news.

So on a laptop and on a tablet held sideways the drawing is the last thing in the rail, under the
places they watch. It was under the flood-risk summary in the wide column from 16 Oct. Measured
afterwards at 1,440 px: the two stacks now end 1,757 px and 1,891 px down, within 8% of each
other, where the rail had been less than half the height of the column beside it.

The card is still written twice - once at the foot of the page, which is what a phone and a tablet
held upright read, and once in the rail - and `app/globals.css` hides whichever copy the width does
not want, so nobody meets it twice. **The phone is provably untouched by this:** the only change to
the page a phone builds is where a `display: none` wrapper sits in it, which no layout and no
screen reader can see. Confirmed in a browser at 360 px, 1,024 portrait, 1,180 landscape, 1,440 and
1,920, in both the ordinary and the Warning state, with no sideways scroll anywhere.

One rule changed with it. At Warning or Evacuate the single column pushes the drawing to the end,
so that nothing is ever put between the alert and SOS (safety rule 1); that rule now names the
card's own mark rather than the band it happens to sit in, so moving it again cannot leave it in
front of the button.

**Four unit tests were failing on `main` and are now fixed.** None of them was a bug in the app;
all four were tests left behind by work that had landed since, which is what happens while the
runner is believed broken (see the 18 Nov entry above).

- The dam-release form was checked against the API it had on 9 Nov. An operator is no longer told
  which level to send - the owner chose on 9 Nov that they get the same three an admin has - and
  the dam chooser now appears for whoever holds more than one reservoir rather than for an admin.
  Rewritten against what the form does, with a second reservoir added so the chooser is tested on
  both screens.
- The map's "my places" button was checked against its behaviour before 25 Oct. Since then the
  place chosen on this device has a pin of its own, so somebody with nothing saved still has one
  place of theirs and the button takes the same tour, with that one stop, rather than framing a
  wide box around the point.
- The scan that keeps the app from ever labelling a time "Thailand time" (the owner, 13 Oct) now
  lifts for `.sitrep`, as it already did for `.about`. A situation report is printed, filed and
  forwarded (A18), so it carries a stated period, and a period on a document that leaves the app
  with no zone on it is a real ambiguity rather than noise. The rule is about the app's own clock
  and its time labels, which is everywhere else.

## 2026-11-19 - A river's cross-section in a gauge's details card, and three levels made public

The owner, 19 Nov 2026: a river gauge's details "should also have the illustrate of the river bed
with river live level with the line of the watch, alert and evacuate, similar to the one in Nara
flood, the river level like should be animated like water wave realistic"; and, the same day,
"This is for the Details view of the river gauges, not within the map view itself. Keep the same
animated icon in the map view."

**Where it is.** The drawing is in the gauge's details card alone (`components/map/GaugeSection.tsx`,
geometry in `lib/gauge-section.ts`). The mark on the map and the small pop-up are untouched: the
map keeps the animated glass of `lib/gauge-marks.ts`, which has to stay small enough to read at 48
of them at once.

**Three levels now leave the database as numbers.** `station_status()` has graded the public map on
`public.gauge_levels` since 21 Oct but deliberately let nothing out of it but the word and the bank
level, and a cross-section cannot be drawn from a word. So the function now also returns
`watch_m`, `warning_m` and `evacuate_m` - the very ones the status word was decided on, so the
picture and the word cannot disagree - plus `levels_provisional`, true unless an admin has reviewed
that gauge's own row (migration `..a10_gauge_levels_public`). Nothing else from `gauge_levels`
leaves: not who set them, not when, not the method. These are not personal data; the risk they
carry is being read as an authority's published thresholds, which the card answers in words: they
are provisional, chosen from past floods at that station, and an admin can change any of them.

**What is measured and what is drawn are kept apart.** The water level and the three levels are
figures, drawn and also printed in metres above mean sea level. The shape of the channel and the
depth of the bed are a drawing - Jaga has no surveyed section for these stations - so the bed
carries no height and the card says the shape is not a survey. A station that has gone quiet is
drawn as an empty channel with its last reading as a grey dashed line, never as water still
standing in it.

**Colour.** The three lines wear the alert palette, as the explainer's own gauge figure already
does, and each is named beneath the drawing with its level's icon and its height: never colour
alone. The water keeps the map's water colour at every height, because a reading is a measurement
and not an alert (safety rule 2), and the card still says so in words.

**The movement.** Two sine bands of different length and speed drift across each other at the
surface, with a light sheen and a glint along the crest; the body rises into place once when the
card opens. Both lengths divide the drawing's width, so each loop ends where it began and no seam
shows. Nothing moves under `prefers-reduced-motion`, and the whole thing is CSS on one SVG - no
canvas, no animation frame, nothing per gauge on the map.

**The frame is the station's levels, not its reading** (added the same day, after the owner stepped
through the stations: "the animated cross sections looks the same just numbering under it
changes"). The first scale took the reading into its own span, so every station's water came out at
much the same height and only the figures moved - the picture carried no information at all. The
frame is now set by that station's Watch, Warning, Evacuate and bank, which lands the three lines
in about the same place at every station and leaves the water free to sit where the river really
stands against them: low at a river well under its Watch, near the top at one close to its bank.
Two stations can therefore be compared by eye, which is the whole point of stepping through them. A
reading outside the levels still stretches the frame to hold it - a figure that fell off the
drawing would read as "not reached" - but only by as much as that takes. The drawn bed sits well
below the lowest level for a related reason: drawn any shallower, a river simply sitting at its
ordinary level came out as a sliver and read as a dry bed. And the water's height is a CSS
transition, so stepping to the next station slides the surface up or down to its new level instead
of cutting to it.
**The bank carries its own height on the drawing** (the owner, same day: "the river bank level
number should be at the bank in the illustration"). The top of the ground now has the bank level
written on it, at the left, whether or not the bank is a level of its own; where the bank IS the
Evacuate level - which is the usual case - the alert line already runs at that height, so no second
line is drawn over it and the words beneath say that the two are the same. The reading's own figure
moved clear of it.

**What is still not real is the shape**, and that is deliberate. The owner asked whether the bed
and bank widths could make the section realistic. Jaga has no surveyed cross-sections; the only
elevation data in reach is FABDEM at 30 m, which cannot resolve a channel 20-50 m wide and carries
a vertical error of 1 to several metres - the same size as the whole Watch-to-Evacuate band it
would be drawn against. A section cut from it would contradict the gauge's own levels while looking
authoritative, which is worse than an honest schematic. The one real figure within reach is the
river's **width** at each station, from the OSM river-body layer the map already draws
(`pipeline/rivers`); setting the drawn channel's width from it would make a wide river look wide
and a canal narrow, with the vertical still carried entirely by measured levels. Offered to the
owner, not built.

## 2026-11-19 - The map's tiles join the reservoir in the rail, and the rail runs the page's length

**The owner, pointing at the row of seven map tiles:** "In the home page, bring these boxes, to
fill the right side, for laptop view, under manage your watch places." So on a laptop and on a
tablet held sideways the way into the map is now the first thing under the places they watch, with
the reservoir's drawing after it. The tiles ran the full width of the page for exactly one day,
between 18 and 19 Nov. They go back to two abreast, which is how they are drawn everywhere else.

The tiles and the reservoir are each written twice - once in the rail and once in the run of
blocks a phone reads - and the CSS hides whichever copy the width does not want. **It cannot be
done by placing the band in the grid instead.** A grid row is as tall as its tallest item, so a
band placed under the rail starts where the long column beside it ends, which is the very gap this
was meant to close. This is the second time that rule has decided the shape of this page.

**And it bit again, one step further down.** With the tiles and the reservoir in it, the rail
became the taller of the two stacks - so the safe places, which sat in the grid row underneath,
waited for it, and the page grew a 600 px hole in the middle of its left-hand side. The emptiness
had simply moved from one side to the other. The fix is that the rail now spans both rows
(`grid-row: 1 / span 2`): the wide column and the safe places under it are one continuous read,
the rail runs down beside both, and "share Jaga" is the last line across the whole page. Spanning
costs nothing, because a spanning item only stretches its rows when it is taller than all of them
together, which the rail is not. The page lost 400 px of height in the bargain.

What is left is the ordinary shape of a page with a sidebar: the rail ends while the safe places
carry on. That emptiness is at the foot of the page and beside a list, not a hole in the middle of
the reading.

**Two things worth writing down.** The tiles' heading had a fixed `id`, which two copies on one
page would have duplicated; it is made with `useId` now. And the reservoir's drawing has carried
five duplicated SVG ids since 16 Oct, for the same reason - checked in a browser on 19 Nov, and
every gradient and clip still resolves from the hidden copy, so nothing is broken. The same was
confirmed for the tiles' one clip path.

## 2026-11-19 - The monitoring card says what it means, and names the rivers that are rising

**Decision.** The live monitoring card gains one sentence under its headline saying what the
readings mean for the reader, and the line that counts rising rivers now names them and offers a
way to see them on the map. The owner, of "4 river gauges have risen over the last three hours":
"should the user worry? or it's still ok, or should follow jaga closely" - and then "if they want
to know which rivers have been rising then they can click to see those river gauge name and should
also blink and little bit zoom in in the map but yet still need to show the user's area, so the
user can understand that how far the gauges are from their location".

**What it means for the reader.** One sentence, chosen from the card's own four states and nothing
else (`MonitoringMeaning`). Watching: check Jaga more often today, watch the water where you are,
and follow the level Jaga has published. In range: nothing here asks anything of you now, and the
readings can change within hours. Incomplete: the readings are not the whole picture, follow
official warnings as well. Unknown: follow official warnings, and 1784 in an emergency.

Two things it may never say, and tests hold both. **Whether an alert is in force**: this module
cannot know - the level comes from `areaStatus` and is drawn by the hero - so a line here reading
"no alert in your area" would be false for every reader who is under one (safety rule 3). It points
at the published level instead of claiming anything about it. **That anybody is safe**: the quiet
sentence speaks about the readings, as the headline above it does, and says in the same breath that
they can change, because instruments that lag by hours going nowhere is not an all-clear (rule 10).

**Which rivers.** The small home-screen feed now carries a station's id and name, but only for one
at or near its bank or one that has risen in the last three hours (`worthNaming`): naming all
forty-odd in three languages to answer a question about four of them is exactly what that feed
exists to avoid, and when the rivers are quiet it costs nothing. The names are listed under the
sentence that counted them, at most five with the rest said as a number - the count stays the
authority, so a river cannot leave the screen for want of a name.

Only ever under the rising sentence. With a river at or near its bank the line above is about that
river, and a press lights the banks first (`gaugesLit`), so the names and the button are drawn only
when the sentence is the rising one.

**And the frame.** A press now frames the reader's own place beside the marks it lights
(`litFrame`): a frame holding only the stations answers "which rivers" and hides the one thing a
resident wants from it, which is whether those are up the road or in the next province. Only the lit
stations within `GAUGES_NEAR_KM` of that place join the frame; where every one of them is further
off, it holds their place and the nearest station alone, two points, rather than four provinces
saying nothing. With no place saved it is the stations by themselves, as before. Stepping through
stations one at a time inside the open card is unchanged - they have left the group they were shown.

`gaugesLit` gained the three-hour tier between the banks and the week, because that is the order the
words are in. From the home screen the same press is a link, `?focus=gauges&lit=rising`: the map
works out which stations those are from its own full feed when it arrives, so a link that is kept or
passed on gives the rivers that are rising when it is opened and never a stale set.

**Why.** Both halves are the same question - a resident reading a measurement and not knowing what
to do with it. Nothing here computes or suggests a level (safety rule 2).

**Tested.** 112 unit cases over the frame, the tiers, the naming rule and the words, and one e2e on
the home screen. Lint, typecheck and build clean. The map's own press is covered by unit tests on
its pure parts only; `[data-map-lit]` e2e in this repo is timing-flaky and was not added to.

## 2026-11-19 - The bell's season queue restored on jaga-dev, and two dam tests that had gone stale

**The policy suite was run against jaga-dev** (646 passed, 46 failed, 19 files, 3 h 47 m; the suite
is serial by config). Most failures were already known or environmental. Three were not, and all
three are fixed or explained here.

**1. jaga-dev had silently lost the bell's `seasonNotice` queue.**
`20261110011000_s11_season_notice` rebuilt `admin_attention()` whole to add that queue, because the
function returns a set and there is no way to add one from outside. `20261109120100_a12_dam_operator`
carries the EARLIER timestamp and redefines the same function without the queue - and it was applied
to jaga-dev by hand AFTER S11 on 10 Nov, so its `create or replace` reverted it. Module 11 kept
writing `season_notices` rows; no admin's bell ever counted them. Caught by
`tests/rls/season-state.test.ts` ("stands in the bell as a silent queue") and confirmed against the
live function definition before anything was changed.

Fixed by a new migration, `20261119101500_s11_season_notice_restore`, whose body is S11's block
copied word for word and whose timestamp puts it after every migration that touches that function.
The migrations were always in the right order, so a rebuild from scratch was never wrong and this
file is a no-op there. Applied to jaga-dev; `season-state.test.ts` is 17/17 again.

**Why a migration and not a hand-run `create or replace`.** The same drift would come back the next
time anything re-applied A12, and nothing in the repo would record that jaga-dev had ever differed
from its own ledger. An applied migration is never edited (conventions), so the repair is a new one.

**2. `dam.test.ts` "storage above normal high is watchful, not a release".** Not a fault. An admin
gave Bang Lang a full share of its own on 27 Oct through the dam-levels console (`dam_levels_set` in
`audit_log`, 85% / 80%, from none). 85% of 1,589.8 Mm3 is 1,351, which is below its normal high of
1,454.4, so with that share set every storage above normal high is also "full and rising" and grades
as a release - correctly, by the S7 rule. The test, written 15 Oct, assumed the dam had no such
share. It now takes the two keys off inside its own rolled-back transaction, so it tests the
normal-high rule alone and reads the same on a fresh database and on jaga-dev.

**3. `dam-reservoirs.test.ts` "gives a visitor every dam, with how often each reports".** Also not a
fault. The test (15 Oct) expected Khlong Sadao to report more than 10 tambons, its OSM channel
through Hat Yai. Seed 43 (27 Oct) gave the five gauge-less reservoirs an overflow zone and switched
`dams.geometry_source` to `spill`, where Khlong Sadao covers 2 - the 17 OSM rows still sit in
`dam_tambons` beside them. `dam_map()` counts only the rows at the dam's own source, which is right.
The fixed figures are replaced by the contract: every dam's count equals its rows at its own
`geometry_source` and never borrows another source's, and a reservoir with a zone reports at least
one. Both suites 51/51.

**Left open, deliberately.** The four `gauges.test.ts` failures are `column "rose_days" does not
exist` - migration `20261110170000_s11_gauge_week_trend` is another session's in-flight work and is
not mine to apply. The 9 `weather-advisory` failures are the same thing for
`20261108090000_a16_weather_advisory`. Both are tracked as pending in the handoff notes.

## 2026-11-19 - Three things the owner found on the live map and home screen

The owner walked the live site and wrote up five findings. Two were not what they looked like, and
are recorded here with the other three so the next session does not re-diagnose them.

**1. A rain cloud took the tap meant for the gauge under it.** "My first click opened the reservoir
rainfall popup instead of the gauge, and on a phone this will be worse." The clouds are HTML markers
held over the canvas, so the map cannot weigh them against its own marks the way it weighs those
against each other - the cloud simply gets the event first. Worse, the marker's box is 46x58 and the
cloud fills only the top 46x28 of it; the rest is the air its rain falls through, and while it took
taps it was a transparent lid over whatever the map had drawn beneath.

Decided: the cloud's body alone answers the pointer, and a gauge, safe place or dam mark exactly
under the point keeps its own tap - the handler answers `false` and the event is left alone to reach
the map. Rejected the owner's first suggestion, hiding the weather icons whenever the gauge layer is
on: the cloud over a person's own place is worth having, and the bug was the tap order, not the
cloud. Also left the gauge icon's size alone - see point 5.

**2. A tap said everything twice.** "The popup stays open over the map while the side panel shows the
same values." A tap pins the pop-up and opens the card both. Decided by where the card lands: on a
console it is the rail beside the map and over a full-screen map it is a dialog on top of it, so
there the pop-up only repeated the card and covered the map to do it, and the tap now closes it. On
a phone the card opens below the map and nothing scrolls to it (option C, 20 Oct 2026), so there the
pop-up is the tap's only answer in sight and stays pinned. A pointer's hover pop-up is untouched: on
a laptop that is how a mark says what it is before anyone commits a click to it.

**3. TMD's paragraph is folded on the home screen.** "Show the headline, affected period and one
summary line, and put the full paragraph behind 'อ่านเพิ่ม'." Done, with one refusal: no summary line
of our own. TMD's words are quoted verbatim precisely because trimming an authority's warning can
change what it says, so the box ends the quote and we never do - `line-clamp` leaves every word in
the page, and a screen reader reads the notice whole whether it is folded or not (CLAUDE.md, "UI
text"). The card carries TMD's own title, the period TMD gave it, and TMD's opening lines. The
weather screen still opens the newest notice.

**4. The cross-section's missing threshold lines are an unapplied migration, not a data gap.** The
station the owner opened (X.73A, บ้านบองอ) showed "ยังแสดงเส้นระดับเกณฑ์ของสถานีนี้ไม่ได้", and it is
not that station: `20261119093000_a10_gauge_levels_public` is what lets `watch_m`, `warning_m` and
`evacuate_m` out of `station_status()`, and it is not applied on jaga-dev, so *every* station says
so. X.73A has levels of its own - 26.20 / 26.50 / 26.80, bank 28.51
(`pipeline/thresholds/gauge_levels.md`) - and the three lines will draw the moment it is applied,
with no code change and nothing asked of RID. It must go on after `20261110170000_s11_gauge_week_trend`.
Waiting on the owner.

**5. Still open: a hydrograph, and the gauge icon's size.** `station_status()` returns one latest
value and `rise_3h`, so there is no series for the 24-hour sparkline the owner wants; it needs a new
public read of `observations` per station, held to the same discipline as the levels above. Left for
after the cross-section work. On the icon: the glass is 21 px across at full size and 10.5 px at
zoom 7, under the 48 px target - but `icon-allow-overlap` is on and 32 gauges are in view at that
zoom, so enlarging it there trades a hard tap for a pile of overlapping glasses. `TAP_SLACK` (12 px,
measured over 665 taps on 20 Oct) is the lever, and moving it deserves the same measurement rather
than a guess. Neither changed; both are the owner's call.

## 2026-11-19 - The cross-section shows the real channel: its bed, its two banks and its width

The owner, after the first drawing: "if we have the river bed and bank width would be nice to make
the illustration of the cross section more realistic" - and then "build it, as much as we can, of
course no need to wait for the surveyed data."

**What turned out to be within reach, without a survey.** ThaiWater's own station record already
carries three heights Jaga had never read: `left_bank_m`, `right_bank_m` and `ground_level_m`. That
is a real bed level at 39 of the 52 gauges and two real bank heights at 50 - and the two banks
differ at half of them, sometimes by metres, which decides which side the river leaves first. The
width comes from the river-body layer's own OpenStreetMap cache, measured across the water its
mappers digitised: a transect perpendicular to the channel's centreline, five of them 40 m apart,
median. New pipeline module `pipeline/gauge_section`, new table `public.gauge_channel`, seed 62,
and four more columns out of `station_status()`.

**What is deliberately missing.** A station whose river OSM maps only as a centreline gets **no
width at all** - the river-body layer buffers those lines by a made-up half-width so narrow
channels still show on the map, and publishing that as a measurement would be a figure nobody took.
That is why only 17 of the 52 have one. A bed that is not below its bank is dropped. Every missing
figure stays missing, and a station with none keeps the schematic channel and the old words.

**Still not a survey, and the card still says so.** What is measured is now the depth, the two bank
heights and the width; the *shape of the sides* between bed and bank is still a drawing. A station
with figures says where they came from - the station record and OpenStreetMap - instead of the
blanket "this shape is a drawing".

**Two honesty problems the real figures created, and how they are answered.** A channel can be far
deeper than the band its levels sit in: nineteen metres at one gauge against a level span of one,
which drawn to scale would squeeze Watch, Warning and Evacuate into the top tenth of the picture -
the part a reader has to read. So a bed deeper than the frame can hold is **cut**: the drawing
shows the channel going down past a break and the words give the bed's real height. And a bed below
mean sea level is normal here, not an error - thirteen of the gauges have one - so negative heights
are drawn as they are.

**A bug worth recording.** The width reached the database and the card's words but not the drawing:
`riverSection` took `width` while a gauge carries `channelWidth`, so TypeScript saw an optional
field that was simply never passed and said nothing. Both channels were still drawn at the default
middling size and the picture looked plausible. The input field is named `channelWidth` now, and a
test asserts a narrow river is drawn narrower than a wide one.

## 2026-11-19 - The rest of the policy suite, run in batches: three gates had quietly lost a clause

The remaining 44 RLS files were run against jaga-dev in six batches (the whole suite is serial by
config and takes 3 h 47 m; batches of eight are minutes each). Everything below came out of that.

**Three repairs applied, all the same shape as the morning's `admin_attention()` one**: a function
rewritten whole by a later migration, from a copy that predated a clause, dropping it silently while
the ledger said all was well.

**1. A pending unit's POC phone was readable again** (`20261119121500_a19_poc_phone_target_verified`).
20261015135100_a10_security_fixes required both halves - the target unit verified AND the caller a
verified unit in shared coverage. 20261109120100_a12_dam_operator rewrote the function for the
dam-only exclusion keeping only the caller's half, and A19 carried it forward. From 9 Nov a verified
rescue unit could reveal the POC phone of a unit that had merely registered. Narrow - the caller had
to be a verified team and every reveal is logged - but a personal number the security pass had
deliberately closed. The clause is back on top of A19's body.

**2. A team's dispatchers had lost every read the team has**
(`20261119124500_a19_team_people_read_again`), and this was the worst of the day.
20261024090000_t1_unit_members put the 24 Oct decision - a verified team's people count as the team
- into `is_verified_authority()`, `covers_tambon()` and `my_unit_for_sos()` by replacing
`u.user_id = auth.uid()` with `unit_access(u.id, auth.uid())`. A12 rewrote all three from their
pre-24-October bodies and A19 rewrote them again; both kept `user_id = auth.uid()`. So from 10 Nov
only the account that registered a unit could act for it: a dispatcher asking for the case board got
"Verified authorities only", a case they were sent was "not in your coverage", and a requester's
phone would not open for them. During a flood that is a team's people locked out while the owner's
phone is the only way in. `unit_access()` is back in all three, with A19's office-only and A12's
dam-only lockdowns untouched - the A19 gate check still passes 34/34. `reveal_poc_phone()` was
deliberately NOT widened, because 20261024090000 did not widen it either.

**3. The admins' feedback trends screen could not be opened at all**
(`20261119113000_a15_feedback_trends_note`): `max(o.note)` over `feedback_outcomes.note`, which is
jsonb, so the statement never parsed and every call raised. `feedback_outcomes.code` is its primary
key, so the aggregate was only ever there for the group by; two correlated subqueries read that one
row instead. Dead since 27 Oct.

**4. Also tightened** (`20261119114000_a15_feedback_no_direct_update`): `feedback` granted insert and
update to anon and authenticated with no policy for either, so `update feedback set rating = 1`
changed nothing and reported success. Nothing could be altered and nothing leaked, but a refused
write should say so. Both grants revoked; select and delete keep their policies. Every write the app
makes goes through a definer function.

**One finding left for the owner, not applied** (`20261119131500_a14_weak_merge_keeps_needs`, in the
repo and ready). `submit_sos()` merges a repeat two ways: a STRONG match (same account or case
token) updates the needs, a WEAK one (same phone or device only) keeps the case's position on
purpose - a shared phone may be a different household, and a pin that moved could send a boat to the
wrong house - and never touched the needs at all. So a family that sent a rescue SOS and then asked
from the same phone for medicine had that request reduced to one line in the event log: the case's
needs stayed empty. Safety rule 1 says such a repeat merges into the case as an update, and an
update that drops what was asked for is not one, so the migration adds the needs to the case's list
(never replacing it, note and "what we have left" only filling a gap). It is left unapplied because
it is the SOS path and the judgement is the owner's: on a shared phone one household's needs can
join another's open case, against the request vanishing. `tests/rls/supplies.test.ts` stays red until
that is decided, which is the honest state.

**Tests corrected where the test was the thing that was wrong** (seven cases, six files). None was a
policy fault: `access` expected the organizations table to hold only its fixture and every SOS to be
its own two; `war-room` counted every `read_sos_board` and `read_people` row jaga-dev has ever held
(139 and 60) instead of the ones it adds; `reservoir-alarm` expected one armed reservoir where seed
44 arms five and an admin armed Bang Lang, and inserted an alert with no `issued_by`, which has been
NOT NULL since 29 Sep and so never could have passed; `feedback` passed a fixture object where a
uuid belonged and read `authority_units` as a user who cannot see it; `line` built its alerts with
the `channels` default, which stopped including LINE on 22 Oct when LINE became opt-in per alert, so
the reach came back empty - a case pinning that rule was added; `admin-alarm-watch` compared two rows
whose `created_at` came from the same `now()`, a tie it won or lost run by run.

**Why so many absolute counts.** Every one of these reads a table jaga-dev also holds real rows in.
On a fresh CI database they pass, which is why they were not noticed; on the database the live site
reads they cannot. They now measure what the test itself changed.

**Left failing, and deliberately not touched.** `gauges` (4) wants `rose_days` from
`20261110170000_s11_gauge_week_trend`, `weather-advisory` (9) wants `20261108090000_a16_weather_advisory`
and `sitrep` (5) wants `unit_sitrep()` - three other sessions' migrations, none of them mine to
apply. A version clash was also caught by jaga-bd and settled: `20261119113000` was already applied
under the a15 name, so the gauge-channel file took `20261119115000` rather than rewriting an applied
row.

## 2026-11-19 - Correction: X.73A's bank is 26.80 m, and there was never a discrepancy

The entry above, "Three things the owner found on the live map and home screen", says X.73A has
"bank 28.51". That is wrong, and the 1.7 m disagreement I reported from it does not exist. Recorded
here rather than edited there, as this log is append-only.

The table in `pipeline/thresholds/gauge_levels.md` runs:

    | Zone | Gauge | Watch | Warning | Evacuate (bank) | Highest recorded | Floods | Years | How |

So for X.73A the bank is **26.80 m** - the Evacuate column, which the header labels as the bank -
and **28.51 m is the highest level ever recorded** at that station, the next column along. I read
the record flood as the bank. A river's record standing 1.71 m above its bank is unremarkable at a
station that has flooded twice.

Checked against jaga-dev rather than argued: `gauge_levels.evacuate_m` equals `stations.warning_m`
for **46 of 46 rows** to within 5 mm. Evacuate is the bank by design, and the levels did not
inherit a stale column, which was the thing worth being sure of. There is nothing for the owner to
decide and nothing in either seed to change.

Two further notes, because the error travelled before it was caught:

**It was put to the owner as settled evidence, which is the part that mattered.** A wrong bank
figure handed to a hydrologist reads as a safety finding. It was retracted to the owner and to the
session doing the cross-section work; a third session was sent the retraction without ever having
been told the claim, which it rightly pointed out.

**Two agents agreeing is not evidence.** The cross-section session independently "confirmed" a bank
of 28.50/28.51 from its own output - those were the neighbouring station X.73's figures, not
X.73A's - and so reached the same wrong conclusion from the other direction. Both of us were
reading a number near 28.5 and neither read the column it sat under. The check that settled it was
the header and a count over all 46 rows.

## 2026-11-22 - The week a river has had is drawn as a line, and the bad day that was faking a flood

The owner: "now that we have the records of 7 days water level to tell the user if it's been risen
for the past 7 days, instead of just word we should have a minimal graph to support the wording as
well, trend graphs".

**The chart.** `station_status()` has counted the week since 10 November but kept the series to
itself: it returned how many days the daily low rose and how far it moved in all, and threw the
eight daily lows away. They now come out as `week_floors`, gated exactly as the two counts are -
null unless the whole run of days is there, so a partial week can never be drawn. The card keeps
the sentence first and puts the line under it: the thing the owner wants a reader to see must not
start depending on reading a picture.

**What the chart deliberately does not do.** It carries **no level**. A *daily low* below Watch says
nothing about whether that day's peak was, so drawing the thresholds across this series would be a
claim the series cannot support; the levels stay on the cross-section, which is drawn from the
reading they grade (safety rules 2 and 8). It is one hue, the river's own blue at every height,
never the alert palette - the dam's charts settled that in October. Nothing moves: a line that drew
itself in would be a week that looked like it was still happening. And all eight figures are in the
label, so nothing the line says is gated behind seeing it.

**The scale has a floor of half a metre, because auto-scaling is how you lie with a sparkline.**
Fitted to its own range alone, a river whose daily low wandered two centimetres all week is drawn
climbing the full height of the box and a reader sees a flood in the noise of a still river.
Measured against the real series on jaga-dev: a quiet station wanders 10-15 cm in a week and now
stays visibly flat, while X.119 rising 0.90 m still uses the whole height. The range the box covers
is printed under it, so the shape always has a size attached.

**The bug the picture found, which is the more important half of this.** Drawing the series was
what finally showed it: X.73A read a daily low of **11.31 m on 14 November between two days of
22.3 m**. A river does not fall eleven metres and climb back inside a day. That single dropout was
the whole of the station's `week_change_m`, so the public map was telling residents the Pattani
river at X.73A had **risen 10.91 m in eight days**. It had not moved at all. A sensor fault was
being published as a flood signal, and the counting alone could never have shown it - this is an
argument for the chart that is better than the chart.

So a day whose daily low differs from the day before it *and* the day after it by more than 2 m, in
opposite directions, is treated as the fault it is, and the station becomes unjudgeable for the week
exactly as an incomplete run already does: null, and the card's existing "the week's trend cannot be
read at this station". The bad day is **not** quietly dropped and the figure is **not** corrected,
because either would publish a number nobody measured. Three things make the rule safe: a one-day V
in a series of daily *minima* is an instrument and not a river, so a rise that holds even one more
day is untouched; 2 m sits in open ground between the only two candidates anywhere in twelve days of
every station (X.73A's 10.93 m dropout, and X.113's real 0.62 m of rain, which is left alone); and
the worst a wrong guess can do is make Jaga say "this cannot be read" about a station it could have
read - it can never publish anything, colour a tambon or move a level.

Migration `20261122093000_s11_gauge_week_series`, rebuilt whole from the a10_gauge_channel version
after diffing the live `pg_get_functiondef()` against it. **Not applied**: it changes what the
public map says about a station, and the owner has not been asked yet. Verified read-only against
jaga-dev by running the new body as a plain SELECT - 30 stations judged, 30 with a series, no length
or gate mismatches, and X.73A silent instead of claiming eleven metres.

- 2026-11-22 — **The automatic alarm checked against the live database while the rivers were rising; two gauges armed, LINE given a switch** (the owner: *"now that the water in many rivers are rising, make sure that the automatic warning works properly"*, then *"also check auto alarm for the Reservoirs functioning properly and armed"*, then *"arm X.44 and X.240, and for the LINE Chanel, add in the Alert, that I can switch on or off, as of now make it on as default, but I can switch off anytime"*).

  **The alarm itself works.** 45 checks against jaga-dev in one rolled-back transaction (`.claude/local/alarm-live-check.mjs`, kept): a believable rise publishes at once with its reason, next-update time, 26 tambons and audit row, not a drill, and tells all three admins afterwards; a jump of 6.1 → 9.0 m in 24 minutes is held with a deadline and the gauge marked suspect, so a faulty sensor delays an alert by 20 minutes and can never stop one; one reading, two readings not both over the level, a reading over three hours old, a reservoir over 95% but emptying, and a second alert inside the quiet window all raise nothing, while an Evacuate still escalates past it. The reservoir road does the same at 95% of capacity and names the reservoir's own tambons.

  **X.44 and X.240 armed.** X.44 บ้านหาดใหม่ใน is Hat Yai's key gauge and was not armed, so the zone the whole คลองอู่ตะเบา alarm is keyed to had nobody listening. Its levels were checked before arming and are sound: highest recorded 9.97 m against a Warning of 7.05 m, 0.3% of hours above Watch over 6.7 years, in a channel 10.9 m deep — it reads ~1 m now only because the river is low. **X.240 บ้านหูแร่ had no zone at all**, and `auto_alarm_check()` skips a station without one, so arming it alone would have shown it watched in the console and fired nothing. It stands inside tambon 901112 ทุ่งตำเสา, one of the u_taphao zone's own 30, so it was given that zone — a matter of fact, not a judgement. Both verified to publish, each warning the zone's 30 tambons.

  **LINE is now one of the roads an automatic alert travels, with a switch (`system_settings.auto_alarm.line`, on by default).** An alert an admin publishes carries `channels = {push, line}` because the console ticks them; an alert the alarm published was made inside the database, ticked nothing, took the column default `ARRAY['push']`, and `line_targets_for_alert` then refused the road that was not ticked. So the one alert path with no human in it was the one path that never reached LINE — while 45 accounts have LINE against 11 web-push devices, and 43 people were owed the Kolok alert over LINE in the test. spec.md 6.2 already said the default was "push and LINE", so the schema and the spec had disagreed since the alarm was built. `admin_set_auto_alarm_line()` turns it either way and logs who did; closing it takes LINE off alerts still to come, never one already sent. The sending needed a road of its own, since `line_targets_for_alert` is `admin_only()` and an automatic alert has no admin and no screen: `line_auto_alert_due()` hands the recipients and the words to the gate token, booking each alert once so a second tick tells nobody twice. The delivery figures and the quota are unchanged for either road — `line_record_alert`'s body moved into `line_alert_recorded()`, reachable by no client, and both the admin's road and the tick's call it, so the 70% and 90% marks are identical whoever published. Migration `20261122180000_a10_auto_alarm_line`, commit 7737038, **not applied**: 25 checks passed in a rolled-back transaction.

  **SLA005 ปากรอ is deliberately NOT armed.** The owner asked for a proper warning alert from it; measured first, and it is the tide. Its 36-hour trace shows two highs and two lows a day about 12.4 hours apart; its all-time maximum over 3,652 readings is 0.97 m, which **is** its Evacuate level, and it touched 0.97 at 08:20 on 22 Nov at high tide. It has stood above its Warning for 1.2% of its whole record, where every armed gauge stands above its Warning 0.0% of the time, and Module 11 excludes it as tidal. Armed as it stands it would have published an Evacuate from a high tide. Put to the owner with the figures, who chose to leave it unarmed and have tide-aware levels built first — on the daily minimum rather than the raw reading, which is where its real signal does live: its daily lows rose 0.72 → 0.79 → 0.84 m over those 36 hours. A Warning for that area stays a human decision until then. — The lesson is the one Module 11 already paid for: an estuary gauge's levels cannot be read off the raw stage, and a false Evacuate costs the trust that every later alert depends on.

  **Two gaps the check turned up, with the owner's answers.** (1) **36 of 48 gauges have no zone**, so the alarm can never consider them; the owner chose to zone every one of them, with the list to approve first. Zoning does not arm: `auto_alarm_gauges.enabled` stays a separate tick per gauge, which matters because 8 of the 17 that can be zoned today are tidal or dam-pulse gauges that must not be armed on their raw levels. The remaining 18 stand in no existing zone's tambon and span three river systems, not one, so the zone scheme itself needs a decision rather than an invented lake-shore zone. (2) **Nothing tells anyone when the gauge feed dies** — the alarm needs a reading inside three hours and a dead feed shows only in the passive feed block on /admin/signals. Still to build.

  Also settled: the public map already colours an alert's whole area for every visitor, signed in or not (`public_alert_status` carries every alert with all its tambons, and `alertForTambon` paints them), so "for all users to see, not just the affected users" was already true. What it does not draw is the warning zone's own boundary as one shape; the owner asked for that outline, which is queued.

## 2026-11-22 - The week chart and its sensor guard are applied to jaga-dev

Follow-up to the entry above, the same day. The owner, having seen the drawings: "built and migrate
to jaga dev". Migration `20261122093000_s11_gauge_week_series` is **applied**.

Verified by `pg_get_function_result()` rather than the ledger: `station_status()` went 21 -> 22
columns with `week_floors double precision[]` on the end. 31 of the 48 stations carry a full
eight-day series, with no length or gate mismatches, and the chart is live on jagaapp.org - checked
on a phone at SLA002 คลองหอยโข่ง, whose quiet week is drawn over a range of 2.48 to 2.98 m: exactly
the half-metre scale floor, doing the job it was put there for.

**One thing recorded so nobody later misreads it.** Bangkok rolled over to 23 November the same
night, so X.73A's 14 November dropout fell out of the eight-day window by itself, and the station
now reads -0.07 m. That is the window moving, not the guard firing. After applying, the rule was
re-checked against the raw daily lows independently of the window: it still marks that day CAUGHT,
and still leaves X.113's real 0.62 m of rain alone. The false 10.91 m is gone either way, but it is
the guard, not the calendar, that stops the next dead sensor being published as a flood signal.

## 2026-11-22 - Live CCTV: link out to the agency's own cameras, never embed or host

The owner asked for free live CCTV of water gauges and dams/reservoirs for the four provinces, shown
to users. Searched for a free API, MCP or embeddable feed and found none that is both documented and
free: ThaiWater's API (the one we already use) has no CCTV service; EGAT's cameras are an
undocumented SPA over SignalR and were returning 500; DDPM's `cctv.disaster.go.th` is the real
live-flood-camera network but sits behind a Cloudflare JS challenge and, decisively, forbids being
shown inside another app (`X-Frame-Options: SAMEORIGIN`, `CSP frame-ancestors 'self'`). No source
exposes a per-frame capture time, so we cannot programmatically tell a live frame from a frozen or
offline one.

Given that, and the owner's choices in the session: **link out, do not embed or proxy.** A closable
in-app window (`components/cctv/CameraWindow.tsx`) opens over the page and holds a button that opens
the agency's own site in a new window; it embeds a page only where that page permits framing, which
none of today's sources do, so every source in `lib/cctv.ts` is `embeddable: false`. We rejected
proxying the feeds through our own server (a fragile scrape of a Cloudflare-protected government SPA,
grey-area, and it still cannot prove freshness). The window says plainly the pictures are the
agency's own, may lag/freeze/be offline, are not a Jaga alert and are not a reason not to act
(safety rules 2, 8, 10), and need a connection (rule 7). Shown on the river-gauge details card
(DDPM) and the dam report (EGAT). Commit 509b92d; i18n strings in 827aaf1.

Reason: no free source gives embeddable, timestamped live video for our provinces; a link-out is the
only path that stays $0, adds no scraping/proxy dependency, and cannot present a stale frame as live.

To flip a source to an in-app embed later: confirm its page both allows framing and shows a capture
time, then set `embeddable: true` in `lib/cctv.ts`. Open follow-up (owner's idea, 22 Nov): find free
city-corner / street-level CCTV showing rain-water pooling or flooding, to add the same way.

## 2026-11-22 - A channel on another datum is not drawn at all

The owner, looking at X.67A บ้านนาสีทอง on the live site: "check why this gauge has strange cross
section with 2 sides water level, and fix it".

**What it was.** The drawing was right and the figures were wrong. ThaiWater lists that station's
bank at **39.92 m MSL** - the level Jaga warns against, and the one the Evacuate line is drawn from
- while listing its two bank tops at **6.278 and 6.29** and its bed at **-1.223**. Those last three
are a real channel, about 7.5 m deep, but measured down from the station's own staff gauge rather
than from mean sea level. Two datums in one picture: the reading of 35.41 came out twenty-nine
metres above both bank tops, so the card filled the ground on either side of the channel with
water - a river standing out of its banks on both sides, beside a sentence saying the station was
อยู่ในระดับปกติ. That is the failure that matters here: not an ugly drawing, but a picture
contradicting the words under it, and between the two it is the words that have been checked
(safety rule 10).

**The rule.** A station's listed bank tops and its bank level describe the same physical bank and
must agree. Where the lower bank top sits more than 10 m from the bank level, the whole channel is
dropped - the bed too, because the bed is listed on the datum of the bank tops and keeping it would
be the same mistake with one fewer clue. The station then keeps the schematic channel and the card
says the shape is a drawing, which is already what every station without listed heights does.
Nothing a reader needs is lost: the levels, the reading and the bank were never the part that was
wrong, and the width is untouched because a distance measured across OpenStreetMap's water has no
datum to disagree with.

**Why ten metres is not fine judgement.** Of the 46 stations carrying listed heights, 45 sit within
4.28 m of their bank level and most within 2 m - a surveyed bank top a little above the warning
bank is ordinary - and X.67A sits 33.64 m away. The threshold has an order of magnitude of clear
air on both sides. ThaiWater's own record gives the same answer independently: its `min_bank_m`
matches `min(left_bank_m, right_bank_m)` at every station but this one.

**Fixed in three places, deliberately.** `pipeline/gauge_section` applies the test and no longer
writes the row (seed 62 is 49 rows, not 50); migration `20261122210000_a10_gauge_channel_datum`
deletes it from jaga-dev, which is never rebuilt from the seed; and `lib/gauge-section.ts` applies
it again at drawing time, so a bad row reaching the database from anywhere still cannot draw a
river standing over its banks. The last of the three is the one that matters: the other two are
housekeeping, and a figure this wrong must not depend on a pipeline having been re-run.

Applied to jaga-dev and confirmed on jagaapp.org: X.67A now draws its water inside the channel,
below all three level lines, with the schematic wording and no bank figures.

## 2026-11-23 - A place page carries its own flood-hazard figure, not a fifteenth copy of the template

The fifteen `/flood/<place>` pages were about 285 words each and 77-80% identical to one another,
and Search Console would not index the thinnest of them (`/flood/sadao`, validation failed
14 Nov). Rather than write fifteen new paragraphs, each page now shows the long-term flood-hazard
share of its own tambons, summed from S3's `hazard_by_tambon` (`lib/place-hazard.ts`).

**Why this figure.** It is already computed, already public to read, and it genuinely differs from
place to place: measured against jaga-dev on 23 Nov 2026 it runs from 30% of buildings on Medium or
High land in Bannang Sata to 71% in Mueang Pattani, and all fifteen places have rows.

**It reuses the home screen's card, words and all** (`TambonRiskView`), so no new safety sentence
had to be written and none can drift from the reviewed one: the same eyebrow saying this is the
long-term picture and not today's alert, and the same note that it is an estimate rather than a
forecast (safety rules 8 and 10). A failed read leaves the section off the page entirely; it never
shows a zero, and no class area at all reads as "not stated", never as "no risk" (safety rule 10).

The four class areas, not `land_km2`, are the denominator: they are what the bar draws, and a bar
built on the land area would not fill if a later radar run left them summing to slightly less,
